and such that, for every
y\in F^{*}
,
u_{j}(\Phi(\cdot,y))\leq 1/2
on a set
S_{j}[y]
which is
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-equivalent to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. We thus find that
\Phi(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times F^{*})
is a set of positive volume which is
\mathcal{L}^{n+1}
-contained in
\{u_{j}\leq 1/2\}
. In particular, if
t>1/2
, then
\{u_{j}^{*}>t\}
is
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-disjoint from
\Phi(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times F^{*})
. However, since
\{u_{j}^{*}>t\}
is
\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
, for
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-a.e.
s
,
T[s]\cup\{u_{j}^{*}>t\}
is essentially disconnecting
T=\Phi(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times B_{1}^{n})
; in particular,
\Phi(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times F^{*})
is essentially disconnected by
(T[s]\cup\{u_{j}^{*}>t\})\cap\Phi(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times F^{*})
which, in turn, is
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-equivalent to
T[s]\cap\Phi(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times F^{*})=\Phi(\{s\}\times F^{*})
. We have thus concluded that for
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-a.e.
s\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
\Phi(\{s\}\times F^{*})
is essentially disconnecting
\Phi(\mathbb{S}^{1}\times F^{*})
, a contradiction.

To conclude, up to modify
F
on an
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-null set we can assume that (
3.34
) holds for every
j
and every
y\in F
. Next we set

F_{j}=\Big\{y\in F:\mathcal{H}^{1}(\{z_{j}^{y}>1/4\})\leq\frac{\mathcal{V}(u_{j};T)}{M}\Big\}\,,

(3.35)

and notice that

C(\Phi)\,\mathcal{V}(u_{j};T)\geq\int_{Y}V(z_{j})\geq\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{1}^{n}\setminus F_{j})\,V(1/4)\,\frac{\mathcal{V}(u_{j};T)}{M}\,,

so that, setting
M=\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{1}^{n})\,V(1/4)/2\,C(\Phi)
, we find

\mathcal{H}^{n}(F_{j})\geq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{1}^{n})}{2}\,,\qquad\forall j\,.

(3.36)

Now, if
y\in F_{j}
, then by (
3.34
) there is
s_{j}^{y}\in\mathbb{S}^{1}
such that
z_{j}^{y}(s_{j}^{y})\geq 1/2
, and
s_{j}^{y}
must lie in a non-empty connected component
I_{j}^{y}
of
\{z_{j}^{y}>1/4\}
; by (
3.35
), it must be
\mathcal{H}^{1}(I_{j}^{y})\leq\mathcal{V}(u_{j},T)/M
, so that, if
j
is large enough to ensure
\mathcal{V}(u_{j},T)/M<\mathcal{H}^{1}(\mathbb{S}^{1})
, then there must be
t_{j}^{y}\in\partial_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}I_{j}^{y}
. In particular,
z_{j}^{y}(t_{j}^{y})=1/4
and
{\rm dist}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(s_{j}^{y},t_{j}^{y})\leq\mathcal{H}^{1}(I_{j}^{y})\leq\mathcal{V}(u_{j};T)/M
, which, combined with
z_{j}^{y}(s_{j}^{y})\geq 1/2
, give

\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}|(z_{j}^{y})^{\prime}|^{2}\geq\int_{[s_{j}(y),t_{j}(y)]}|(z_{j}^{y})^{\prime}|^{2}\geq\frac{|z_{j}^{y}(s_{j}^{y})-z_{j}^{y}(t_{j}^{y})|^{2}}{{\rm dist}_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}(s_{j}^{y},t_{j}^{y})}\geq\frac{M}{16\,\mathcal{V}(u_{j};T)}\,.

for every
y\in F_{j}
, and thus, recalling (
3.36
),

\int_{Y}|\nabla z_{j}|^{2}\geq\int_{F_{j}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}_{y}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}|(z_{j}^{y})^{\prime}|^{2}\geq\frac{\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{1}^{n})}{2}\,\frac{M}{16\,\mathcal{V}(u_{j};T)}\,.

Finally,

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j};\Omega)\geq\varepsilon_{j}\,\int_{\Omega}|\nabla u_{j}|^{2}\geq\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{C(\Phi)}\,\int_{Y}|\nabla z_{j}|^{2}\geq c(\Phi,M)\,\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{\mathcal{V}(u_{j};T)}

from which we find
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j};\Omega)\to\infty
(a contradiction) as
\mathcal{V}(u_{j};T)={\rm o}(\varepsilon_{j})
as
j\to\infty
.
∎

4.
Wet and dry soap films as limits of diffused interface soap films

In the section we address the approximation in Allen–Cahn energy of minimizers
(K,E)
of
\Psi_{\rm bk}(v)
in both the wet (
v>0
) and dry (
v=0
) cases. We shall actually be able to work with a slightly more general class of pairs
(K,E)
, namely, we shall work in the class of those
(K,E)\in\mathcal{K}
, with

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}=\Big\{(K,E):\mbox{$K$ is relatively closed and $\mathcal{H}^{n}$-rectifiable in $\Omega$, $E$ is open,}

(4.1)

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82234pt\mbox{$E$ has finite perimeter in $\Omega$, and $\Omega\cap\mathrm{cl}\,(\partial^{*}E)=\Omega\cap\partial E\subset K$}\Big\}\,,

which also satisfy condition (
4.2
) and (
4.3
) below. In Theorem
4.1
we address the wet case (
|E|>0
), while Theorem
4.3
concerns the dry case (
|E|=0
).

Theorem 4.1

(Diffused interface approximation of wet soap films)
.

Let
\mathbf{W}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be compact and such that
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\mathbf{W}
has smooth boundary, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a spanning class for
\mathbf{W}
. Let
(K,E)\in\mathcal{K}
be such that

|E|>0\,,\qquad\mbox{$K\cup E$ is bounded and $\mathcal{C}$-spanning $\mathbf{W}$}\,,\qquad K\cap E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}=\varnothing\,,

(4.2)

and such that there are
c
and
r_{0}
positive such that

\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap B_{r}(x))\geq c\,r^{n}\,,

(4.3)

for every
x\in\mathrm{cl}\,(K)
and
r<r_{0}
.

If
\nu:(0,1)\to(0,\infty)
and
\delta:(0,1)\to(1/2,1)
are such that, as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
,

\nu(\varepsilon)\to|E|\,,\qquad\delta(\varepsilon)\to\delta_{0}\in[1/2,1]\,,

then there are
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0^{+}
and
\{\{u^{j}_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}}\}_{j}\subset{\rm Lip}(\Omega;[0,1])
such that
\{u_{\varepsilon}^{j}>0\}\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and

\displaystyle\mbox{$\{u_{\varepsilon}^{j}\geq\delta(\varepsilon)\}$ is $\mathcal{C}$-spanning $\mathbf{W}$}\,,\qquad\forall\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}\,,

(4.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{j};\Omega)=\nu(\varepsilon)\,,\qquad\hskip 66.86414pt\forall\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}\,,

(4.5)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\sup_{\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}}\|u_{\varepsilon}^{j}-1_{E}\|_{L^{1}(\Omega)}=0\,,

(4.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\sup_{\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{j};\Omega)}{2}=P(E;\Omega)+2\Phi(\delta_{0})\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}})\,.

(4.7)

Moreover, if
\ell
is finite, then
\Upsilon(v,\varepsilon,\delta)
is finite for every
v>0
,
\varepsilon>0
, and
\delta\in(0,1]
, and

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{\delta\in(0,1]}\Upsilon(v,\varepsilon,\delta)\leq\Psi_{\rm bk}(v)\,.

(4.8)

Remark 4.2

(Choice of spanning set for small
\varepsilon
)
.

Theorem
4.1
shows that given any choice of
\delta(\varepsilon)
such that
\delta(\varepsilon)\to[1/2,\delta_{0})<1
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
we are bound to find

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0^{+}}\Upsilon(v,\varepsilon,\delta(\varepsilon))\leq P(E;\Omega)+2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}})<\mathcal{F}_{\rm bk}(K,E)=\Psi_{\rm bk}(v)\,.

where we have applied (
4.7
) to a minimizer
(K,E)
of
\Psi_{\rm bk}(v)
. This explains why, for recovering the expected/correct limit surface tension energy along the collapsed region one has to require
\delta(\varepsilon)\to 1^{-}
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
.

Theorem 4.3

(Diffused interface approximation of dry soap films)
.

Let
\mathbf{W}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
be compact and such that
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\mathbf{W}
has smooth boundary, and let
\mathcal{C}
be a spanning class for
\mathbf{W}
. Let
K
relatively closed in
\Omega
,
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-rectifiable, bounded,
\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
, and such that there are
c
and
r_{0}
positive with

\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap B_{r}(x))\geq c\,r^{n}\,,

(4.9)

for every
x\in\mathrm{cl}\,(K)
and
r<r_{0}
.

If
\nu:(0,1)\to(0,\infty)
and
\delta:(0,1)\to(1/2,1)
are such that, as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
,

\displaystyle\nu(\varepsilon)\to 0^{+}\,,\qquad\delta(\varepsilon)\to\delta_{0}\in[1/2,1]\,,\qquad\frac{\varepsilon}{\nu(\varepsilon)}\to 0\,,

(4.10)

then there are
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0^{+}
and
\{\{u^{j}_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}}\}_{j}\subset(W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}\cap{\rm Lip})(\Omega;[0,1])
such that

\displaystyle\mbox{$\{u_{\varepsilon}^{j}\geq\delta(\varepsilon)\}$ is $\mathcal{C}$-spanning $\mathbf{W}$}\,,\qquad\forall\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}\,,

(4.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{j};\Omega)=\nu(\varepsilon)\,,\qquad\hskip 66.86414pt\forall\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}\,,

(4.12)

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\sup_{\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{j};\Omega)}{2}\leq 2\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K)\,.

(4.13)

Remark 4.4
.

For the necessity of the third condition in (
4.10
), see conclusion (
3.20
) in Theorem
3.4
.

In the proof of both theorems, as well as in the sequel, we will make use of the following elementary lemma:

Lemma 4.5
.

(i):
If
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is open,
X\in C^{\infty}_{c}(A;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, and
f_{t}(x)=x+t\,X(x)
, then there are positive constants
t_{0}
and
C_{0}
depending on
X
only, such that, for every
|t|<t_{0}
,
f_{t}:A\to A
is a diffeomorphism, and for every
w\in W^{1,2}(A;[0,1])
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(w\circ f_{t};A)-\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(w;A)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-t\,\int_{A}\,\varepsilon\,|\nabla w|^{2}+\frac{W(w)}{\varepsilon}\,{\rm div}\,\,X-2\,\varepsilon\,(\nabla w)\cdot\nabla X[\nabla w]\Big|\leq C_{0}\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(w;A)\,t^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\Big|\mathcal{V}(w\circ f_{t};A)-\mathcal{V}(w;A)-t\,\int_{A}\,V(w)\,{\rm div}\,\,X\Big|\leq C_{0}\,\mathcal{V}(w;A)\,t^{2}\,,

(4.14)

(ii):
If
u\in L^{1}(A;[0,1])
and
u
is not constant on
A
, then there are positive constants
\eta_{0}
,
\beta_{0}
and
C_{0}
(depending on
A
and
u
) such that for every
w\in W^{1,2}(A;[0,1])
with
\|u-w\|_{L^{1}(A)}\leq\beta_{0}
and every
|\eta|<\eta_{0}
there is a diffeomorphism
f_{\eta}^{w}:A\to A
with
\{f\neq{\rm id}\,\}\subset\subset A
such that
w_{\eta}=w\circ f_{\eta}^{w}
satisfies

\mathcal{V}(w_{\eta};A)=\mathcal{V}(w;A)+\eta\,,\qquad|\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(w_{\eta};A)-\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(w;A)|\leq C_{0}\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(w;A)\,|\eta|\,.

Proof.

Statement (i) is a standard consequence of the area formula. Concerning statement (ii), we notice that since
u
is not constant in
A
and
V
is strictly increasing on
[0,1]
, it follows that
V(u)
is not constant in
A
. In turn, this implies the existence of
X\in C^{\infty}_{c}(A;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
such that
\int_{A}\,V(u)\,{\rm div}\,X>0
, and then one can argue as in
[
Mag12
, Lemma 29.13, Theorem 29.14]
.
∎

We now prove Theorem
4.1
and Theorem
4.3
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

We start by proving (
4.8
). By Theorem
1.1
,
\ell<\infty
implies the existence of a minimizer
(K,E)
of
\Psi_{\rm bk}(v)
(
|E|=v>0
) which satisfies the assumptions in the first part of the statement. Since
u_{\varepsilon}^{j}
(corresponding to
\nu(\varepsilon)\equiv v
and
\delta(\varepsilon)\equiv 1
) is admissible in
\Upsilon(v,\varepsilon,\delta)
for every
\delta\in(0,1]
and
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}
, we easily deduce (
4.8
). The rest of the proof is divided in four steps, to which we premise the following result and a preliminary remark related to it:

[
Vil09
, Proposition 4.13]
:
If
F
is a Borel set in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that
(a):
\partial F
is countably
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-rectifiable, and
(b):
there are
c^{\prime}
and
r_{0}^{\prime}
positive such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{r}(x)\cap\partial F)\geq c^{\prime}\,r^{n}\,,\qquad\forall x\in\partial F\,,r<r_{0}^{\prime}\,,

(4.15)

then for every Borel set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with

\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial F\cap\partial A)=0\,,

(4.16)

it holds

\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{|(I_{r}(F)\setminus F)\cap A|}{r}=P(F;A)+2\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial F\cap F^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\cap A)\,,

(4.17)

where
I_{r}(F)=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:{\rm dist}(x,F)<r\}
.

A remark on
[
Vil09
, Proposition 4.13]
: In this remark, let us assume
F
is closed and satisfies (a) and (b). We first point out that the open set
F^{c}
satisfies these assumptions also. This is immediate from the fact that a set and its complement share the same topological boundary. We now record three facts to be used later (in each of them,
A
satisfies (
4.16
)). First, by (
4.17
) applied to
F
and the fact that
|F\setminus\mathrm{int}\,F|=|\partial F|=0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{|(I_{r}(F)\setminus F)\cap A|}{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(F;A)+2\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial F\cap F^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\cap A)

(4.18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(\mathrm{int}\,F;A)+2\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial F\cap(\mathrm{int}\,F)^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\cap A)\,.

Second, applying (
4.17
) to
F^{c}
and again using
|F\setminus\mathrm{int}\,F|=0
, we find

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{|I_{r}(F^{c})\cap(\mathrm{int}\,F)\cap A|}{r}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}\frac{|(I_{r}(F^{c})\setminus F^{c})\cap A|}{r}

(4.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(F^{c};A)+2\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial(F^{c})\cap(F^{c})^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\cap A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(\mathrm{int}\,F;A)+2\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial F\cap(\mathrm{int}\,F)^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\cap A)\,.

Third, setting
{\rm sd}_{\partial F}(x)=-{\rm dist}(x,\partial F)
if
x\in F
,
{\rm sd}_{\partial F}(x)={\rm dist}(x,\partial F)
if
x\in F^{c}
, and
f_{\varepsilon}(s)=\mathcal{H}^{n}(A\cap\{{\rm sd}_{\partial F}=\varepsilon\,s\})
for
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we claim that, in the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
in the sense of Radon measures on
\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{L}^{1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{*}}}{{\rightharpoonup}}

\displaystyle\big\{P(\mathrm{int}\,F;A)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial F\cap(\mathrm{int}\,F)^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\cap A)\big\}\,\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(0,\infty)

(4.20)

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\{P(\mathrm{int}\,F;A)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial F\cap(\mathrm{int}\,F)^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\cap A)\big\}\,\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(-\infty,0)\,.

Indeed, setting for brevity
\alpha=\big\{P(\mathrm{int}\,F;A)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial F\cap(\mathrm{int}\,F)^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\cap A)\big\}
, we deduce from (
4.19
) that, that for every
b>0
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{b}\,f_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{b}\mathcal{H}^{n}(A\cap\{{\rm sd}_{\partial F}=\varepsilon\,s\})\,ds=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\int_{0}^{\varepsilon\,b}\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(A\cap\{{\rm sd}_{\partial F}=t\})\,dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b\,\frac{|(I_{\varepsilon\,b}(F)\setminus F)\cap A|}{\varepsilon\,b}\to b\,\alpha\,.

In particular, for every
(a,b)\subset(0,\infty)
we have
\int_{a}^{b}\,f_{\varepsilon}\to(b-a)\,\alpha
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, and a similar argument based on (
4.18
) completes the proof of (
4.20
).

Step one
: We prove that
F=\mathrm{cl}\,(E\cup K)=\mathrm{cl}\,E\cup\mathrm{cl}\,K
satisfies assumptions (a) and (b) of
[
Vil09
, Proposition 4.13]
. To prove this, we begin by showing that

\displaystyle\partial F=\mathrm{cl}\,K\cup\partial E\subset\mathrm{cl}\,\Omega\,.

(4.21)

The containment in
\mathrm{cl}\,\Omega
is trivial by
K\cup E\subset\Omega
, so we compute
\partial F
. Towards this end, by the fact (since
E
is open) that
E
,
\partial E
, and
(\mathrm{cl}\,E)^{c}
partition
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we decompose

\displaystyle\partial F=(\partial F\cap E)\cup(\partial F\cap\partial E)\cup(\partial F\setminus\mathrm{cl}\,E)

(4.22)

and evaluate each term individually. First, since
E
is open and
E\subset F
,

\displaystyle\partial F\cap E=\varnothing\,.

(4.23)

Second, we claim that
\partial E\subset\partial F
, so that

\displaystyle\partial E\cap\partial F=\partial E\,.

(4.24)

To prove
\partial E\subset\partial F
, we must show that if
x\in\partial E
, then

\displaystyle B_{r}(x)\cap F\neq\varnothing\quad\textup{and}\quad B_{r}(x)\setminus F\neq\varnothing\quad\forall r>0\,.

(4.25)

Indeed, if
x\in\partial E
, then
B_{r}(x)\cap E\neq\varnothing
for all
r>0
by definition of
\partial E
, and so
E\subset F
gives the first condition in (
4.25
). For the second, we first claim
x\notin E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}
. Indeed, since
x\in\partial E\subset\partial\Omega\cup K
and
\partial\Omega\cap E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}=\varnothing=K\cap E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}
(due to
E\subset\Omega
, the smoothness of
\partial\Omega
, and our assumption on
K
), we have
x\notin E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}
. Therefore, noting that
E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}=F^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}
(by
|F\setminus E|=|\mathrm{cl}\,K\cup\partial E|\leq|K\cup\partial\Omega|=0
), we find that
x\notin F^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}
. In turn, this implies that
B_{r}(x)\setminus F\neq\varnothing
for all
r>0
as desired, finishing the proof of (
4.25
) and thus (
4.24
). For the last term in (
4.22
), we claim that

\displaystyle\partial F\setminus\mathrm{cl}\,E=\mathrm{cl}\,K\setminus\mathrm{cl}\,E\,.

(4.26)

To see this, we note that by the definition of
F
,
\partial F\setminus\mathrm{cl}\,E=\partial(\mathrm{cl}\,K)\setminus\mathrm{cl}\,E
. Now since
|\mathrm{cl}\,K|\leq|K|+|\partial\Omega|=0
, we have
\mathrm{int}\,(\mathrm{cl}\,K)=\varnothing
, and thus

\displaystyle\partial(\mathrm{cl}\,K)=\mathrm{cl}\,K\setminus\mathrm{int}\,(\mathrm{cl}\,K)=\mathrm{cl}\,K\,.

Thus
\partial F\setminus\mathrm{cl}\,E=\mathrm{cl}\,K\setminus\mathrm{cl}\,E
, which is (
4.26
). We may now conclude the proof of (
4.21
). By (
4.22
), (
4.23
), (
4.24
), and (
4.26
),
\partial F=\partial E\cup(\mathrm{cl}\,K\setminus\mathrm{cl}\,E)=\partial E\cup(\mathrm{cl}\,K\setminus\mathrm{int}\,E)
, so we would be done with (
4.21
) if
\mathrm{cl}\,K\cap\mathrm{int}\,E=\varnothing
. Now since
E
is open,
\mathrm{cl}\,K\cap\mathrm{int}\,E=\varnothing
if and only if
K\cap E=\varnothing
. The latter condition holds since, by the openness of
E
and our assumption
E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\cap K=\varnothing
,
E\cap K\subset E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\cap K=\varnothing
.

As a first consequence of (
4.21
) and
E\cap K=\varnothing
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{int}\,F=\mathrm{cl}\,F\setminus\partial F

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{cl}\,K\cup\mathrm{cl}\,E)\setminus(\mathrm{cl}\,K\cup\partial E)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{cl}\,E\setminus(\mathrm{cl}\,K\cup\partial E)=\mathrm{int}\,E\setminus\mathrm{cl}\,K=E\setminus K=E\,.

(4.27)

Also (
4.21
), the relative closedness of
K
in
\Omega
, and the containment
\Omega\cap\partial E\subset K
give

\Omega\cap\partial F=\Omega\cap(\mathrm{cl}\,K\cup\partial E)=\Omega\cap(K\cup\partial E)=\Omega\cap K\,.

(4.28)

Since
\partial\Omega
is
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-rectifiable, we deduce from (
4.28
) and the fact that
K
is
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-rectifiable that
\partial F=\mathrm{cl}\,K\cup\partial E
is
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-rectifiable, and thus satisfies (a). The validity of (
4.15
) from (b) at every
x\in\partial F\cap\mathrm{cl}\,(K)
is a consequence of assumption (
4.3
). The validity of (
4.15
) at
x\in\partial F\setminus\mathrm{cl}\,(K)\subset\partial E\cap\partial\Omega
can be deduced as follows: with
c
and
r_{0}
as in (
4.3
), if
r<r_{0}
and there is
y\in B_{r/2}(x)\cap\mathrm{cl}\,(K)\neq\varnothing
, then by (
4.3
) and
K\subset\partial F

\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{r}(x)\cap\partial F)\geq\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{r/2}(y)\cap K)\geq c\,(r/2)^{n}\,;

if, instead,
B_{r/2}(x)\cap\mathrm{cl}\,(K)=\varnothing
, then
\Omega\cap\partial E\subset K
and
E
open imply that
B_{r/2}(x)\cap\partial F=B_{r/2}(x)\cap\partial\Omega
, and we conclude by the fact that
\mathcal{H}^{n}(B_{r}(x)\cap\partial\Omega)\geq c_{\Omega}\,r^{n}
for every
r<r_{\Omega}
, provided
r_{\Omega}
and
c_{\Omega}
are suitable positive constants.

Step two
: We would like to apply
[
Vil09
, Proposition 4.13]
to
F
and
A=\Omega
, although doing so would require checking that
\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial\Omega\cap\partial F)=0
, something that is potentially false (e.g., if
\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial\Omega\cap\partial E)>0
). To avoid this difficulty, we “slightly stretch”
K
and
E
as follows. Since
\Omega
has a (bounded) smooth boundary, there is
t_{0}>0
such that if we define
g_{t}:\Omega\to\Omega_{t}:=I_{t}(\Omega)
,
t\in(0,t_{0})
, by setting
g_{t}(x)=x
for
x\in\Omega\cap\{{\rm dist}_{\partial\Omega}>t\}
and

g_{t}(x)=x+\big({\rm dist}_{\partial\Omega}(x)-t\big)\,\nabla{\rm dist}_{\partial\Omega}(x)\,,\qquad x\in\Omega\cap\{{\rm dist}_{\partial\Omega}<t\}\,,

then
g_{t}
is diffeomorphism with
g_{t}\to{\rm id}\,
and
g_{t}^{-1}\to{\rm id}\,
as
t\to 0^{+}
(in every
C^{k}
-norm). Setting
K_{t}=g_{t}(K)
and
E_{t}=g_{t}(E)
we see that
F_{t}=\mathrm{cl}\,(K_{t}\cup E_{t})
satisfies assumptions (a) and (b) of
[
Vil09
, Proposition 4.13]
. Also by (
4.21
) and the fact that
g_{t}^{-1}(\Omega)\subset\Omega
,

\displaystyle\partial(F_{t})\cap\Omega=g_{t}((\mathrm{cl}\,K\cup\partial E)\cap g_{t}^{-1}(\Omega))=g_{t}(K\cap g_{t}^{-1}(\Omega))=K_{t}\cap\Omega\,,

(4.29)

and by (
4.27
),

\displaystyle\mathrm{int}\,F_{t}=E_{t}\,.

(4.30)

Moreover, since
{\rm dist}(g_{t}(x),\partial\Omega)=2\,{\rm dist}(x,\partial\Omega)-t
for every
x\in\Omega\cap\{{\rm dist}_{\partial\Omega}<t\}
, we see that

g_{t}^{-1}\big(\partial\Omega\cap\partial(F_{t})\big)=g_{t}^{-1}(\partial\Omega)\cap\partial F\subset\big\{{\rm dist}_{\partial\Omega}=t/2\big\}\cap\partial K\,.

Since
\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\{{\rm dist}_{\partial\Omega}=t/2\})=0
for a.e.
t\in(0,t_{0})
and
g_{t}^{-1}
is a Lipschitz map we conclude that

\mathcal{H}^{n}\big(\partial\Omega\cap\partial(F_{t})\big)=0\,,\qquad\mbox{for a.e. $t\in(0,t_{0})$}\,.

(4.31)

As a consequence, we may apply (
4.18
) to
F_{t}
with
A=\Omega
: by using (
4.29
) and (
4.30
) to rewrite
\partial F_{t}\cap\Omega
and
\mathrm{int}\,F_{t}
, respectively, for a.e.
t\in(0,t_{0})
and as
r\to 0^{+}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\big|\big(I_{r}(F_{t})\setminus F_{t}\big)\cap\Omega\big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\,\big\{P(E_{t};\Omega)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(\Omega\cap K_{t}\cap E_{t}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}})\big\}+r\,{\rm o}_{t}(1)

(4.32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\,\mathcal{F}_{\rm bk}(K_{t},E_{t})+r\,{\rm o}_{t}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\,\big(1+\omega(t)\big)\mathcal{F}_{\rm bk}(K,E)+r\,\omega_{t}(r)\,,

where
\omega(t)\to 0
as
t\to 0^{+}
and
\omega_{t}(r)\to 0
as
r\to 0^{+}
, and where in the last identity we have used the area formula and
\|g_{t}-{\rm id}\,\|_{C^{1}}\leq C(\Omega)\,t
. By the same logic applied to
F_{t}^{c}
with (
4.19
) replacing (
4.18
), we also have

\displaystyle\big|I_{r}(F_{t}^{c})\cap F_{t}\cap\Omega\big|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\,\big(1+\omega(t)\big)[P(E;\Omega)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\cap\Omega)]+r\,\omega_{t}(r)

(4.33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r\,\big(1+\omega(t)\big)P(E;\Omega)+r\,\omega_{t}(r)\,,

where in the second line we have used our assumption that
K\cap E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}=\varnothing
. Finally, we notice that

\mbox{$\Omega\cap F_{t}=K_{t}\cup E_{t}$ is $\mathcal{C}$-spanning $\mathbf{W}$}\,;

(4.34)

indeed, for every
\gamma\in\mathcal{C}
,
\gamma(\mathbb{S}^{1})\cap(K_{t}\cup E_{t})=g_{t}((g_{t}^{-1}\circ\gamma)(\mathbb{S}^{1})\cap(K\cup E))
where this last set is non-empty since
g_{t}^{-1}\circ\gamma
is homotopic to
\gamma
relatively to
\Omega
.

Step three
: We prove that for a.e.
t<t_{0}
(
t_{0}
depending on
(K,E)
), every
M>0
, and every
\varepsilon>0
we can define

u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t}\in{\rm Lip}(\Omega;[0,1])\,,\qquad\{u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t}>0\}\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\,,

(see (
4.40
)) in such a way that
\{u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t}\geq\delta(\varepsilon)\}
is
\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
and

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)-|E|\big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,\big(t\,P(E)+\varepsilon\,M\big)\,,

(4.35)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}|u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t}-1_{E}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,\big(t\,P(E)+\varepsilon\,M\big)\,,

(4.36)

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)}{2}-P(E;\Omega)-2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\omega(1/M)+\omega(t)+\omega_{t,M}(\varepsilon)\,,

(4.37)

where
C
depends on the data of the theorem, and where
\omega(r)
(
\omega_{a}(r)
) denotes a generic non-negative increasing function such that the limit
\omega(r)\to 0
(
\omega_{a}(r)\to 0
) as
r\to 0^{+}
holds at a rate that depends on the data of the theorem (and on the parameter
a
). The construction goes as follows. By the normalization (
1.10
) of
W
, the Allen–Cahn profile
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};(0,1))
defined by
-\eta^{\prime}=\sqrt{W(\eta)}
on
\mathbb{R}
,
\eta(0)=1/2
,
\eta(-\infty)=1
and
\eta(+\infty)=0
, is such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}}(\eta^{\prime})^{2}+W(\eta)=2\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\sqrt{W(\eta)}\,|\eta^{\prime}|=2\,\int_{0}^{1}\sqrt{W}=2\,.

Starting from
\eta
, for every
M>0
we can easily construct
\eta_{M}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};[0,1])
with
\{\eta_{M}=1\}=(-\infty,0]
,
\{\eta_{M}=0\}=[M,\infty)
and such that

\displaystyle\int_{\eta_{M}^{-1}(\delta_{0})}^{\infty}(\eta_{M}^{\prime})^{2}+W(\eta_{M})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\Phi(\delta_{0})+\omega(1/M)

(4.38)

\displaystyle\int^{\eta_{M}^{-1}(\delta_{0})}_{-\infty}(\eta_{M}^{\prime})^{2}+W(\eta_{M})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(1-\Phi(\delta_{0}))+\omega(1/M)\,,

(4.39)

where
\omega(1/M)\to 0
as
M\to\infty
. Let
\eta_{M}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}
be the translation of
\eta_{M}
such that
\eta_{M}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}(0)=\delta(\varepsilon)
, and similarly for
\delta_{0}
. Corresponding to
\varepsilon
,
M
, and
t
positive, with
t<t_{0}
, we now set

u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t}(x)=\eta_{M}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}\,\Big(\frac{{\rm sd}_{F_{t}}(x)}{\varepsilon}\Big)\,,\qquad x\in\Omega\,.

(4.40)

In this way,
u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t}\in{\rm Lip}(\Omega;[0,1])
with compact support on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. Since
\{u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t}\geq\delta(\varepsilon)\}=\Omega\cap F_{t}=K_{t}\cup E_{t}
, by (
4.34
) we deduce that
\{u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t}\geq\delta(\varepsilon)\}
is
\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
. Next we notice that since
0=V(0)\leq V(t)\leq V(1)=1
for every
t\in[0,1]
, by combining the area formula (to deduce
|E\Delta E_{t}|\leq C\,P(E)\,t
) with (
4.32
) we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega\setminus E_{t})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\big|\big(I_{\varepsilon\,M}(F_{t})\setminus F_{t}\big)\cap\Omega\big|

(4.41)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon\,M\,\big(1+\omega(t)\big)\mathcal{F}_{\rm bk}(K,E)+\varepsilon\,M\,\omega_{t}(\varepsilon\,M)\,.\,

Similarly, using instead (
4.33
), we find

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega\cap E_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|E_{t}|-\mathrm{O}\big(\big|I_{\varepsilon\,M}(F_{t}^{c})\cap F_{t}\cap\Omega\big|\big)

(4.42)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|E|-\mathrm{O}\big(C\,P(E)\,t-\varepsilon\,M\,\big(1+\omega(t)\big)P(E;\Omega)-\varepsilon\,M\,\omega_{t}(\varepsilon\,M)\big)\,.

Together, (
4.41
) and (
4.42
) give (
4.35
); we deduce (
4.36
) similarly. Finally, by the coarea formula,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)}{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\frac{\big[(\eta_{M}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{\prime}(z/\varepsilon)\big]^{2}+W(\eta_{M}^{\delta(\varepsilon)}(z/\varepsilon))}{\varepsilon}\,\,\,\mathcal{H}^{n}\big(\Omega\cap\{{\rm sd}_{F_{t}}=z\}\big)\,dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\big[\big((\eta_{M}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{\prime}\big)^{2}+W(\eta_{M}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})\big]\,f_{\varepsilon}^{t}\,

where we have set for brevity

f_{\varepsilon}^{t}(s)=\mathcal{H}^{n}\big(\Omega\cap\{{\rm sd}_{F_{t}}=\varepsilon\,s\}\big)\,,\qquad s>0\,.

Since, by (
4.20
) and (
4.29
)-(
4.30
), for a.e.
t\in(0,t_{0})
, as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
,

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}^{t}\,\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{*}}}{{\rightharpoonup}}

\displaystyle\big\{P(\mathrm{int}\,F_{t};\Omega)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial F_{t}\cap(\mathrm{int}\,F_{t})^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\cap\Omega)\big\}\,\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(0,\infty)

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\{P(\mathrm{int}\,F_{t};\Omega)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(\partial F_{t}\cap(\mathrm{int}\,F_{t})^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\cap\Omega)\big\}\,\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(-\infty,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{P(E_{t};\Omega)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K_{t}\cap E_{t}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\cap\Omega)\big\}\,\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(0,\infty)

\displaystyle\qquad+\big\{P(E_{t};\Omega)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K_{t}\cap E_{t}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\cap\Omega)\big\}\,\mathcal{L}^{1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(-\infty,0)\quad\mbox{as $\varepsilon\to 0^{+}$}

and
\big((\eta_{M}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{\prime}\big)^{2}+W(\eta_{M}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})
converges uniformly to
\big((\eta_{M}^{\delta_{0}})^{\prime}\big)^{2}+W(\eta_{M}^{\delta_{0}})
in
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R};[0,1])
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
, we find in particular that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\,\big[\big((\eta_{M}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})^{\prime}\big)^{2}+W(\eta_{M}^{\delta(\varepsilon)})\big]\,f_{\varepsilon}^{t}\,ds

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{P(E_{t};\Omega)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K_{t}\cap E_{t}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}}\cap\Omega)}{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\big((\eta_{M}^{\delta_{0}})^{\prime}\big)^{2}+W(\eta_{M}^{\delta_{0}})\,ds+\omega_{t,M}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{P(E_{t};\Omega)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K_{t}\cap E_{t}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\cap\Omega)}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\big((\eta_{M}^{\delta_{0}})^{\prime}\big)^{2}+W(\eta_{M}^{\delta_{0}})\,ds+\omega_{t,M}(\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathcal{F}_{\rm bk}(K_{t},E_{t})[\Phi(\delta_{0})+\omega(1/M)+\omega_{t,M}(\varepsilon)]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\big\{P(E_{t};\Omega)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K_{t}\cap E_{t}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\cap\Omega)\big\}[1-\Phi(\delta_{0})+\omega(1/M)+\omega_{t,M}(\varepsilon)]\,,

where
\omega_{t,M}(\varepsilon)\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
and we have used (
4.38
)-(
4.39
). Since, as noticed in (
4.32
) and (
4.33
),

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\rm bk}(K_{t},E_{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\omega(t))\,\mathcal{F}_{\rm bk}(K,E)\quad\textup{and}

\displaystyle P(E_{t};\Omega)+2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K_{t}\cap E_{t}^{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}\cap\Omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\omega(t))P(E;\Omega)

we conclude that (
4.37
) holds.

Step four
: We conclude the proof. Given
j\in\mathbb{N}
, we can find
t_{j}\to 0^{+}
and
M_{j}\to\infty
(as
j\to\infty
) depending on the data of the problem, and then
\varepsilon_{j}
depending on
t_{j}
,
M_{j}
and the data of the problem, such that,
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0^{+}
as
j\to\infty
and, for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}
,

w_{\varepsilon}^{j}=u_{\varepsilon}^{M_{j},t_{j}}\in{\rm Lip}(\Omega;[0,1])

with
\{w_{\varepsilon}^{j}>0\}\subset\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
\{w_{\varepsilon}^{j}=1\}

\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
, and

\displaystyle\max\Big\{\big|\mathcal{V}(w_{\varepsilon}^{j};\Omega)-|E|\big|\,,\int_{\Omega}|w_{\varepsilon}^{j}-1_{E}|\,,\Big|\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(w_{\varepsilon}^{j};\Omega)}{2}-P(E;\Omega)-2\,\Phi(\delta)\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}})\Big|\Big\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\frac{1}{j}\,.

(4.43)

Next,
we use
|E|>0
to deduce that
u=1_{E}
is non-constant in the open set
A=\Omega
: by taking
j
large enough we can thus apply Lemma
4.5
to
w=w_{\varepsilon}^{j}
and with
\eta=\nu(\varepsilon)-\mathcal{V}(w_{\varepsilon}^{j};\Omega)
for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}
; denoting by
f_{j}
the resulting diffeomorphism, we set
u_{\varepsilon}^{j}=w_{\varepsilon}^{j}\circ f_{j}
, and notice that, by (
4.43
),
u_{\varepsilon}^{j}
satisfies (
4.4
), (
4.5
), (
4.6
), and (
4.7
).
∎

Next we turn to the proof of Theorem
4.3
. This will be the first situation where we make use of the properties of the minimizers in the diffused Euclidean isoperimetric problem
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)
, consisting of the minimization of
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u):=\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
under the volume constraint
\mathcal{V}(u):=\mathcal{V}(u;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})=v
; see Appendix
A
for more details.

Proof of Theorem
4.3
.

Since the assumption “
|E|>0
” was not used in the proof of Theorem
4.1
until the definition of
u_{\varepsilon}^{j}
in step four, we notice that, for a.e.
t\in(0,t_{0})
and for every
M
and
\varepsilon
positive, if we define
u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t}
as in (
4.40
), then (
4.35
) and (
4.37
) combined with
E=\varnothing
give

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\,\varepsilon\,M\,,

(4.44)

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)}{2}-2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\omega(1/M)+\omega(t)+\omega_{t,M}(\varepsilon)\,,

(4.45)

Given
M>0
, there is
\varepsilon_{*}=\varepsilon_{*}(M)>0
be such that
C\,\varepsilon\,M<\nu(\varepsilon)
for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}
. In particular, for a.e.
t\in(0,t_{0})
and every
M>0
and
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)\leq\nu(\varepsilon)

(4.46)

and (
4.45
). Now, given
w>0
, let us denote by
\zeta_{\varepsilon,w}
the unique minimizer of the diffused isoperimetric problem

\Theta(w,\varepsilon)
(see Appendix
A
), and let us consider for a.e.
t\in(0,t_{0})
,
M>0
,
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}(M)
,
\alpha>0
, and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}

u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t,\alpha,\beta}=\max\big\{u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t},\zeta_{\varepsilon,w_{*}}\circ\lambda_{\alpha}\circ\tau_{\beta}\big\}\,,\quad x\in\Omega\,,

where
\tau
is a fixed unit vector in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
,
\tau_{\beta}(x)=x-\beta\,\tau
and
\lambda_{\alpha}(x)=x/\alpha
(
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
), and where we have set
w_{*}=w_{*}(\varepsilon,M,t)=\varepsilon+\nu(\varepsilon)-\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)
; we immediately see that

u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t,\alpha,\beta}\in(W_{\rm loc}^{1,2}\cap{\rm Lip})(\Omega;[0,1])\,,

and that

\mbox{$\{u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t,\alpha,\beta}\geq\delta(\varepsilon)\}$ is $\mathcal{C}$-spanning $\mathbf{W}$}\,,

since it contains
\{u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t}\geq\delta(\varepsilon)\}
, as well as that

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}\big(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t,\alpha,\beta};\Omega\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)+\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{\varepsilon,w_{*}}\circ\lambda_{\alpha};\Omega)

(4.47)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)+\big\{1+C(n,W)\,|\alpha-1|\big\}\,\Theta(\varepsilon,w_{*})\,.

Now, since
\{u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t}>0\}
is compactly contained in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, we have, uniformly on
|\alpha-1|<1/2
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\beta\to\infty}\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t,\alpha,\beta};\Omega)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)+\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{\varepsilon,w_{*}}\circ\lambda_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)+\alpha^{n}\,\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{\varepsilon,w_{*}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)+\alpha^{n}\,\big(\varepsilon+\nu(\varepsilon)-\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t};\Omega)\big)\,.

This last expression, evaluated at
\alpha=1
, is an
\varepsilon
above
\nu(\varepsilon)
. In summary, for for a.e.
t\in(0,t_{0})
, for every
M>0
,
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}(M)
,
\beta>\beta_{*}(M,t,\varepsilon)
there is
\alpha(\beta,\varepsilon)\leq 1
, with
\alpha(\beta,\varepsilon)\to 1
as
\beta\to\infty
(uniformly in
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}
), such that

\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M,t,\alpha(\beta,\varepsilon),\beta};\Omega)=\nu(\varepsilon)\,.

We can now pick
t_{j}\to 0^{+}
,
M_{j}\to\infty
,
\varepsilon_{j}=\min\{\varepsilon_{*}(M_{j}),\varepsilon^{*}_{j}\}\to 0^{+}
(where
\varepsilon^{*}_{j}
is such that the error
\omega_{t,M}
appearing in (
4.45
) satisfies
\omega_{t_{j},M_{j}}(\varepsilon_{j}^{*})\to 0^{+}
),
\beta_{j}=\beta_{*}(M_{j},t_{j},\varepsilon_{j})
,
\alpha_{j}(\varepsilon)=\alpha(\beta_{j},\varepsilon)
, and define, for every
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{j}
,

u_{\varepsilon}^{j}=u_{\varepsilon}^{M_{j},t_{j},\alpha_{j}(\varepsilon),\beta_{j}}\in(W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}\cap{\rm Lip})(\Omega;[0,1])

so that
\{u_{\varepsilon}^{j}\geq\delta(\varepsilon)\}
is
\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
,
\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{j};\Omega)=\nu(\varepsilon)
, and combining (
4.45
) with (
4.47
) such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\varepsilon}^{j};\Omega)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K)+\omega(1/M_{j})+\omega(t_{j})+\omega_{t_{j},M_{j}}(\varepsilon_{j}^{*})

\displaystyle+\big\{1+C(n,W)\,|\alpha_{j}(\varepsilon)-1|\big\}\,\Theta(\varepsilon,w_{j}(\varepsilon))\,,

where
w_{j}(\varepsilon)=w_{*}(\varepsilon,M_{j},t_{j})=\varepsilon+\nu(\varepsilon)-\mathcal{V}(u_{\varepsilon}^{M_{j},t_{j}};\Omega)
. Now, since
[
MR22
]
implies

\sup_{0<w\leq 1}\Theta(\varepsilon,w)\leq C(n,W)\,\varepsilon^{n/(n+1)}\,,\qquad\forall\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}(n,W)\,,

we conclude from
\omega_{t_{j},M_{j}}(\varepsilon_{j}^{*})\to 0^{+}
and
\alpha_{j}(\varepsilon)\to 1
(uniformly in
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{*}
) that (
4.13
) holds.
∎

5.
Lagrange multipliers of diffused interface soap films

The following theorem is one of the key results of our analysis, as it provides an upper bound on the size of the Lagrange multipliers
\lambda_{j}
appearing in (
1.1
) – precisely, we show that
\varepsilon_{j}\,\lambda_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
. This information, which is of course interesting in itself, is also useful in the proof of the existence of minimizers of
\Upsilon(v,\varepsilon,\delta)
(and the inclusion of the possibility that
\mathbf{v}_{j}<v_{j}
in the statement is needed in that proof). We notice that our analysis does not touch the very interesting problem of understanding the validity of positive lower bounds on the
|\lambda_{j}|
’s. Intuitively, one would indeed expect that they cannot be too small: indeed, since (
1.1
) is compatible with convergence to Plateau-type singularities, it should not be possible to identify it as a too close approximation of the standard Allen–Cahn equation (for which convergence to Plateau-type singularities is indeed impossible).

Theorem 5.1

(Lagrange multipliers estimate)
.

If
\mathbf{W}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is compact,
\mathcal{C}
is a spanning class for
\mathbf{W}
,
v_{j}
,
\varepsilon_{j}
, and
\delta_{j}
are sequences with

v_{j}\to 0^{+}\,,\qquad\varepsilon_{j}\to 0^{+}\,,\qquad\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{v_{j}}\to 0^{+}\,,\qquad\delta_{j}\to\delta_{0}\in[1/2,1]\,,

as
j\to\infty
, and
u_{j}
are minimizers of
\Upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
for some
\mathbf{v}_{j}\in(0,v_{j}]
such that

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j};\Omega)\leq\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})\,,

(5.1)

then

\lim_{j\to\infty}\varepsilon_{j}\,\lambda_{j}=0\,,

(5.2)

where
\lambda_{j}
is the Lagrange multiplier in the inner variation Euler–Lagrange equation for
u_{j}
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
\varepsilon_{j}\,\lambda_{j}
admits a limit, and then prove (
5.2
) by finding a subsequence
j_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
such that
\varepsilon_{j_{N}}\,\lambda_{j_{N}}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
. Setting for the sake of brevity
{\rm ac}_{\varepsilon}(u)=\varepsilon\,|\nabla u|^{2}+W(u)/\varepsilon
, we are going to achieve this by making a suitable choice of
X\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
in the Euler–Lagrange equation

\int_{\Omega}{\rm ac}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j})\,{\rm div}\,X-2\,\varepsilon_{j}\,\nabla u_{j}\,\cdot\,\nabla X[\nabla u_{j}]=\lambda_{j}\,\int_{\Omega}V(u_{j})\,{\rm div}\,X

(5.3)

satisfied by
u_{j}
. Since the argument is long, it is convenient to first give an overview of it. In step one we prove the convergence of
\mathrm{ac}_{\varepsilon_{j}}j(u_{j})\,\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\Omega
to
2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K
, where
K
is a minimizer of
\ell
, and characterize
K
as the limit of super/sub-level sets of the
u_{j}
’s. In step two we blow-up near a regular point of
K
, say,
0
, and identify
r_{N}\to 0^{+}
and
j_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
such that
K
, at a scale
r_{N}
near
0
, is approximately flat and is “sandwiched” between two regions
F_{N}^{+}
and
F_{N}^{-}
on each of which
u_{j_{N}}
concentrates a
\Phi(\delta_{0})
-amount of Allen–Cahn energy per unit area (compare with (
5.19
)). In step three we consider the blow-ups
\tilde{u}_{N}
of
u_{\varepsilon_{j_{N}}}
near
0
at scale
r_{N}
, and prove that they converge to a double transition from
0
to
\delta_{0}
on each side of
K
near
0
, along a length scale
\sigma_{N}\to 0
(compare with (
5.25
)). In preparation to further blow-up
\tilde{u}_{N}
at the scale
\sigma_{N}
we need to identify first a suitable thin cylinder for performing such blow-up (see the definition of
J_{N}
in step four), and then suitable heights that locate the two transitions from
0
to
\delta_{0}
(see the definition of
s_{N}
and
t_{N}
in step five). The resulting blow-ups
\mathbf{u}_{N}
of
\tilde{u}_{N}
are defined in step five, where their local convergence to on one-dimensional Allen–Cahn profile is proved (see (
5.42
)), and used to readily infer that
\varepsilon_{j_{N}}\,\lambda_{j_{N}}\to 0
by testing the rescaled Euler–Lagrange equation (
5.3
) for
\mathbf{u}_{N}
on a carefully selected vector field.

Step one
: By the assumptions on
\{(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})\}_{j}
, Theorem
4.3
gives

2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\ell\geq\limsup_{j\to\infty}\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})\geq\limsup_{j\to\infty}\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j};\Omega)\big/2\,.

(5.4)

In particular,
\sup_{j}\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j};\Omega)<\infty
, so that if we extract a subsequence and denote by
\mu
the weak-star limit of
|\nabla(\Phi\circ u_{j})|\,\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\Omega
as
j\to\infty
, then, by Theorem
3.4
, the Borel subset of
\Omega
defined by

K=\big\{x\in\Omega:\theta_{*}^{n}(\mu)(x)\geq 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\big\}

is such that
K
is
\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
and
\mu\geq 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K
. Combining this last inequality with (
5.4
) and (
MM
) we find that
K
is a minimizer of
\ell
(thus
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-equivalent to
S={\rm spt}\,\mathcal{H}^{n}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K
) and

\displaystyle 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\ell=\lim_{j\to\infty}\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})=\lim_{j\to\infty}\Upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})=\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j};\Omega)\big/2\,;

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mu=2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K={\rm w^{*}}\!\!\lim_{j\to\infty}|\nabla(\Phi\circ u_{j})|\,\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\Omega={\rm w^{*}}\!\!\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{{\rm ac}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j})}{2}\,\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\Omega\,.\hskip 28.45274pt

(5.6)

We now notice that, thanks to the minimality property in
\ell
,
K
can actually be characterized as a partition limit. To see this, let us recall the construction used in the proof of Theorem
3.4
. There, given
N\in\mathbb{N}
we applied Lemma
3.1
on the interval
I_{0}^{N}=[\delta_{0}-(1/2N),\delta_{0}-(1/N)]
to find
\{t_{j}^{N}\}_{j}\subset I_{0}^{N}
such that, setting
E_{j}^{N}=\{u_{j}>t_{j}^{N}\}
, then
\{\Omega\cap\partial^{*}E_{j}^{N}\}_{j}
admitted a partition limit
S_{0}^{N}
with the property that

\mbox{$S_{0}^{N}$ is $\mathcal{C}$-spanning $\mathbf{W}$ and is $\mathcal{H}^{n}$-contained in $K_{N}=\{\theta_{*}^{n}(\mu)\geq 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0}-1/N)\}$}\,,

(5.7)

compare with (
3.25
) and (
3.26
). Having proved that in the present case
\mu=2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K
, we see that
K_{N}=K
, and therefore, by (
5.7
), that

\ell=\mathcal{H}^{n}(K)\geq\mathcal{H}^{n}(S_{0}^{N})\geq\ell\,.

Hence
S_{0}^{N}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{H}^{n}}}{\subset}K
implies
S_{0}^{N}\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{H}^{n}}}{=}K
, that is, for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

K\overset{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathcal{H}^{n}}}{=}\bigcup_{k}\{\Omega_{k}\cap\bigcup_{i}\partial^{*}U_{N,i}[\Omega_{k}]\}\,,

(5.8)

where
6
6

6

Should
\{\Omega_{k}\}_{k}
be a disjoint family – something it is definitely not! – (
5.8
) would imply, for each
N\neq N^{\prime}
and each

, the existence of a bijection

so that

is Lebesgue equivalent to

for every

, and we could thus drop the

\Omega_{k}
intersects countably many different
\Omega_{k^{\prime}}
’s, and it seems there is no obvious way to trivialize the interaction between
k
and
N
in the building up of
K
.

\{\Omega_{k}\}_{k}
is as in (
2.9
), and
\{U_{N,i}[\Omega_{k}]\}_{i}
is the limit of the essential partitions
\{U_{N,i}^{j}[\Omega_{k}]\}_{i}
of
\Omega_{k}
induced by
\Omega\cap\partial^{*}E_{j}^{N}
in the sense that for every

, and

, we have

as

. In particular, since, by (

),

is

-equivalent to
\{u_{j}^{*}=t_{j}^{N}\}
, we find that

\mbox{$\Omega_{k}\cap\partial^{*}U_{N,i}^{j}[\Omega_{k}]$ is $\mathcal{H}^{n}$-contained in $\{u_{j}^{*}=t_{j}^{N}\}$}\,,

(5.9)

where
t_{j}^{N}\to t_{0}^{N}\in I_{0}^{N}
as

.

Step two
: From this step onward, we focus our analysis near a regular point of
K
. More precisely, since minimizers of
\ell
are Almgren minimizing sets in
\Omega
, by
[
Alm76
]

K
is a smoothly embedded minimal surface in a neighborhood of
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-a.e. of its points. In particular, setting

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{r}=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}:\mbox{$|x_{i}|<r$ for $i=1,...,n+1$}\big\}=(-r,r)^{n+1}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}^{n}_{r}=\mathbf{Q}_{r}\cap\{x_{n+1}=0\}=(-r,r)^{n}\times\{0\}\,,

denoting by
\mathbf{p}
the projection of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
onto
\{x_{n+1}=0\}
, and up to a rigid motion, we can assume that
0\in K
and that there are
r_{0}>0
and a smooth solution to the minimal surfaces equation
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbf{Q}^{n}_{r_{0}};(-r_{0},r_{0}))
with
f(0)=0
,
\nabla f(0)=0
, and

\mathbf{Q}_{r}\cap K=\big\{x\in\mathbf{Q}_{r}:x_{n+1}=f(\mathbf{p}(x))\big\}\,,\qquad\forall r\in(0,r_{0})\,.

Let us now consider the epigraph and subgraph of
f
in
\mathbf{Q}_{r}
, that is, let us consider

{\rm Epi}(f;r)=\big\{x\in\mathbf{Q}_{r}:x_{n+1}>f(\mathbf{p}(x))\big\}\,,\qquad{\rm Sub}(f;r)=\big\{x\in\mathbf{Q}_{r}:x_{n+1}<f(\mathbf{p}(x))\big\}\,,

so that
\{{\rm Epi}(f;r_{0}),{\rm Sub}(f;r_{0})\}
is the essential partition of
\mathbf{Q}_{r_{0}}
induced by
K
. By (
5.8
) and by the smoothness of
f
, for each
N
there are
k_{N}
and
i_{N}^{+}\neq i_{N}^{-}
such that

0\in\Omega_{k_{N}}\cap\partial^{*}U_{N,i_{N}^{\pm}}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]\,.

Proceeding inductively in
N
, we can pick
r_{N}<\min\{r_{0},r_{1},...,r_{N-1}\}
so that

\lim_{N\to\infty}r_{N}=0\,,\qquad\mathbf{Q}_{2\,r_{N}}\subset\subset\Omega_{k_{N}}\,,\qquad\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\partial\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}})=0\,,

(5.10)

and thus

\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap U_{N,i_{N}^{+}}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]={\rm Epi}(f;r_{N})\,,\qquad\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap U_{N,i_{N}^{-}}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]={\rm Sub}(f;r_{N})\,.

In particular, for every
N
, as
j\to\infty
we have

\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap U_{N,i_{N}^{+}}^{j}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]\to{\rm Epi}(f;r_{N})\,,\qquad\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap U_{N,i_{N}^{-}}^{j}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]\to{\rm Sub}(f;r_{N})\,.

(5.11)

We now prove two additional properties of
U_{N,i_{N}^{\pm}}^{j}
, see (
5.15
) and (
5.16
) below, which will be used to suitably select
\{j_{N}\}_{N}
such that
j_{N}\to\infty
as
N\to\infty
:

First
, setting for brevity

\alpha_{N,j}^{\pm}=\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap U_{N,i_{N}^{\pm}}^{j}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]}\frac{{\rm ac}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j})}{2}\,,

and noticing that, thanks to (
5.9
), we can argue as in the proof of (

) in Theorem
3.4
– see, in particular, (

) – we prove that

\displaystyle\liminf_{j\to\infty}\alpha_{N,j}^{\pm}\geq\liminf_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap U_{N,i_{N}^{\pm}}^{j}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]}|\nabla(\Phi\circ u_{j})|

(5.12)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\geq\Phi(t_{0}^{N})\,P(U_{N,i_{N}^{\pm}}[\Omega_{k_{N}}];\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}})\geq\Phi\big(\delta_{0}-(1/2\,N)\big)\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}})\,.

Since (
5.6
) and (
5.10
) give

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\alpha_{N,j}^{+}+\alpha_{N,j}^{-}\leq 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}})\,,

(5.13)

by applying (
5.12
) with

and by (
5.13
) we find

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\alpha_{N,j}^{+}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}})-\liminf_{j\to\infty}\alpha_{N,j}^{-}

(5.14)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big\{2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})-\Phi\Big(\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{2\,N}\Big)\Big\}\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}})

By similarly applying (
5.12
) with
m=1
in combination with (
5.13
), we conclude that,

\displaystyle\Phi\Big(\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{2\,N}\Big)\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\liminf_{j\to\infty}\alpha_{N,j}^{\pm}\leq\limsup_{j\to\infty}\alpha_{N,j}^{\pm}

(5.15)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big\{2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})-\Phi\Big(\delta_{0}-\frac{1}{2\,N}\Big)\Big\}\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}})\,.

Second
, by (
5.9
), since each
U_{N,i}^{j}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]
is essentially connected, for each

,

and

,

\mbox{$U_{N,i}^{j}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]$ is $\mathcal{L}^{n+1}$-contained either in $\{u_{j}>t_{j}^{N}\}$ or in $\{u_{j}<t_{j}^{N}\}$}\,.

Setting

\mathbf{Q}_{r}^{+}=\mathbf{Q}_{r}\cap\{x_{n+1}>0\}\,,\qquad\mathbf{Q}_{r}^{-}=\mathbf{Q}_{r}\cap\{x_{n+1}<0\}\,,

since
f(0)=0
,

, and the smoothness of

for every

, we see that if

is
\mathcal{L}^{n+1}
-contained in

, then, by

and (

),

\displaystyle|\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{+}|\,r_{N}^{n+1}-C\,r_{N}^{n+2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap{\rm Epi}(f;r_{N})|\leq|\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap U_{N,i_{N}^{+}}^{j}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]|+{\rm o}_{j}^{N}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\{u_{j}>t_{j}^{N}\}|+{\rm o}_{j}^{N}\leq\frac{\mathcal{V}(u_{j};\Omega)}{V(1/4)}+{\rm o}_{j}^{N}={\rm o}_{j}^{N}\,,

where
{\rm o}_{j}^{N}\to 0
as

at a rate depending on

. In particular, up to further decrease the value of

(so to have

in place of

, we have proved that for each

, if

is large enough depending on

, then

\displaystyle\mbox{$U_{N,i_{N}^{\pm}}^{j}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]$ is $\mathcal{L}^{n+1}$-contained in $\{u_{j}<t_{j}^{N}\}$}\,.

(5.16)

Step three (selection of
\{j_{N}\}_{N}
and first blow-up)
: Using the estimates proved in step two, we can diagonally extract a subsequence
j_{N}\to\infty
as

such that, if we set

F_{N}^{\pm}=\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap U_{N,i_{N}^{\pm}}^{j_{N}}[\Omega_{k_{N}}]\,,\qquad\sigma_{N}=\frac{\varepsilon_{j_{N}}}{r_{N}}\,,\qquad w_{N}=\frac{v_{j_{N}}}{r_{N}^{n+1}}\,,

then the following holds: first,
\sigma_{N}\to 0
and
w_{N}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
; second, by (
5.11
),

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\max\Big\{\frac{|F_{N}^{+}\Delta{\rm Epi}(f;r_{N})|}{r_{N}^{N+1}},\frac{|F_{N}^{-}\Delta{\rm Sub}(f;r_{N})|}{r_{N}^{N+1}}\Big\}=0\,;

(5.17)

third, by (
5.6
) and (

) (which yield
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j};\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}})\to 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}
)),

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}})}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}}\frac{{\rm ac}_{\varepsilon_{j_{N}}}(u_{j_{N}})}{2}=2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,;

(5.18)

fourth, by (
5.15
),

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}})}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap F_{N}^{\pm}}\frac{{\rm ac}_{\varepsilon_{j_{N}}}(u_{j_{N}})}{2}=\Phi(\delta_{0})\,;

(5.19)

and, finally, by (
5.16
),

\displaystyle\mbox{$\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap\partial^{*}F_{N}^{\pm}$ is $\mathcal{H}^{n}$-contained in $\{u_{j_{N}}^{*}=t_{j_{N}}^{N}\}$}\,,

\displaystyle\mbox{$F_{N}^{\pm}$ is $\mathcal{L}^{n+1}$-contained in $\{u_{j_{N}}^{*}<t_{j_{N}}^{N}\}$}\,.

If we now set
\eta_{r}(y)=r\,y
(
y\in\mathbf{Q}_{1}
) and

G_{N}^{\pm}=\frac{F_{N}^{\pm}}{r_{N}}\subset\mathbf{Q}_{1}\,,\qquad\delta_{N}=t_{j_{N}}^{N}\in I_{0}^{N}\,,\qquad\tilde{u}_{N}=u_{j_{N}}\circ\eta_{r_{N}}\,,

then
\delta_{N}\to\delta_{0}
as
N\to\infty
, while

and (

\displaystyle|G_{N}^{+}\Delta\mathbf{Q}^{+}_{1}|\leq\frac{|F_{N}^{+}\Delta{\rm Epi}(f;r_{N})|}{r_{N}^{n+1}}+\frac{|{\rm Epi}(f;r_{N})\Delta\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}^{+}|}{r_{N}^{n+1}}\leq\frac{|F_{N}^{+}\Delta{\rm Epi}(f;r_{N})|}{r_{N}^{n+1}}+C\,r_{N}\,,

and an analogous estimate for
G_{N}^{-}
, so that,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}|G_{N}^{\pm}\Delta\mathbf{Q}^{\pm}_{1}|=0\,,

(5.20)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\mathbf{Q}_{1}\cap\partial^{*}G_{N}^{\pm}$ is $\mathcal{H}^{n}$-contained in $\{\tilde{u}_{N}^{*}=\delta_{N}\}$}\,,

(5.21)

\displaystyle\mbox{$G_{N}^{\pm}$ is $\mathcal{L}^{n+1}$-contained in $\{\tilde{u}_{N}<\delta_{N}\}$}\,.

(5.22)

Similarly, taking into account that
\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap\mathbf{Q}_{r})/(2r)^{n}\to 1
as

, that
r_{N}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
, and that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}\cap F_{N}^{\pm}}{\rm ac}_{\varepsilon_{j_{N}}}(u_{j_{N}})=r_{N}^{n}\,\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}\cap G_{N}^{\pm}}{\rm ac}_{\varepsilon_{j_{N}}/r_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N})

we deduce from (

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big|\Phi(\delta_{0})-2^{-n}\,\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};G_{N}^{\pm})}{2}\Big|=\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big|2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})-2^{-n}\,\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};\mathbf{Q}_{1})}{2}\Big|=0\,.

(5.23)

Since
V(t)\geq c\,t^{2(n+1)/n}
for some
c=c(W)>0
, we have

v_{j_{N}}\geq\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{r_{N}}}u_{j_{N}}^{2(n+1)/n}=r_{N}^{n+1}\,\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}\tilde{u}_{N}^{2(n+1)/n}\,,

so that

in

; hence, taking also (

) and (

then

, and, moreover,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}|\tilde{u}_{N}^{+}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{-}}|=\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}|\tilde{u}_{N}^{-}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{+}}|=0\,.

(5.25)

We also record for future use that, thanks to (

), it holds

\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}{\rm ac}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N})\,{\rm div}\,Y-2\,\sigma_{N}\,\nabla\tilde{u}_{N}\,\cdot\,\nabla Y[\nabla\tilde{u}_{N}]=\frac{\lambda_{j_{N}}\,\varepsilon_{j_{N}}}{\sigma_{N}}\,\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}V(\tilde{u}_{N})\,{\rm div}\,Y\,,

(5.26)

for every
Y\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{Q}_{1};\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
.

Step four (identification of the second blow-up)
: In this step, we show that for every
N
large enough, there is
\xi_{N}\in\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{n}
such that, setting

Q_{N}=\xi_{N}+\mathbf{Q}_{\sigma_{N}/2}^{n}\subset\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{n}\,,\qquad J_{N}=Q_{N}\times(-1,1)

(5.27)

we find

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\max\Big\{\frac{1}{\sigma_{N}^{n}}\int_{J_{N}}|\tilde{u}_{N}^{+}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{-}}|\,,\frac{1}{\sigma_{N}^{n}}\int_{J_{N}}|\tilde{u}_{N}^{-}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{+}}|\Big\}=0\,,

(5.28)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\max\Big\{\Big|2\Phi(\delta_{0})-\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};J_{N})}{2\,\sigma_{N}^{n}}\Big|,\Big|\Phi(\delta_{0})-\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};J_{N}\cap G_{N}^{\pm})}{2\,\sigma_{N}^{n}}\Big|\Big\}=0\,.\hskip 28.45274pt

(5.29)

(We shall actually prove the existence of
many

\xi_{N}
with these properties). To begin with, let us define a sequence
\beta_{N}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
by setting

\beta_{N}=\max\Big\{\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}|\tilde{u}_{N}^{+}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{-}}|\,,\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}}|\tilde{u}_{N}^{-}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{+}}|\Big\}\,,

and, denoting by
M_{N}
the integer part of
2/\sigma_{N}
, we consider a collection

\{Q_{N,i}\}_{i=1}^{M_{N}^{n}}\,,\qquad Q_{N,i}=\xi_{N,i}+\mathbf{Q}_{\sigma_{N}/2}^{n}\,,\qquad\xi_{N,i}\in\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{n}\,,

of disjoint open cubes contained in
\mathbf{Q}^{n}_{1}
, with side length
\sigma_{N}
, and such that
\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathbf{Q}^{n}_{1}\setminus\bigcup_{i}Q_{N,i})\leq C(n)\,\sigma_{N}
(of course, if
2/\sigma_{N}
itself is an integer, then such cubes can be chosen so that
\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathbf{Q}^{n}_{1}\setminus\bigcup_{i}Q_{N,i})=0
). Noticing that

\Big|1-(M_{N}\,\sigma_{N})^{n}/\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{n})\Big|=\Big|1-(M_{N}\,\sigma_{N})^{n}/2^{n}\Big|\leq C(n)\,\sigma_{N}\,,

(5.30)

we consider the open cylinders
\{J_{N,i}\}_{i=1}^{M_{N}^{n}}
,
J_{N,i}:=Q_{N,i}\times(-1,1)\subset\mathbf{Q}_{1}
, so that

|J_{N,i}|=2\,\sigma_{N}^{n}\,,\qquad\Big|\mathbf{Q}_{1}\setminus\bigcup_{i}J_{N,i}\Big|\leq C(n)\,\sigma_{N}\,,

and let

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}=\Big\{1\leq i\leq M_{N}^{n}:\int_{J_{N,i}}|\tilde{u}_{N}^{+}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{-}}|\leq\,\sqrt{\beta_{N}}\,\sigma_{N}^{n}\Big\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2}=\Big\{1\leq i\leq M_{N}^{n}:\int_{J_{N,i}}|\tilde{u}_{N}^{-}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{+}}|\leq\,\sqrt{\beta_{N}}\,\sigma_{N}^{n}\Big\}\,.

On combining (
5.30
) with

\displaystyle(M_{N}^{n}-\#\,\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1})\,\sigma_{N}^{n}\,\sqrt{\beta_{N}}\leq\sum_{i\not\in\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}}\int_{J_{N,i}}|\tilde{u}_{N}^{+}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{-}}|\leq\beta_{N}\,,

we find

0\leq 1-\frac{\#\mathcal{G}_{N}^{m}}{M_{N}^{n}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{\beta_{N}}}{M_{N}^{n}\sigma_{N}^{n}}\leq C(n)\,\sqrt{\beta_{N}}\,,\qquad m=1,2\,,

(5.31)

provided
N
is large enough. In particular,

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\#(\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2})}{M_{N}^{n}}=1\,,

(5.32)

so that, for
N
large, the vast majority of the cubes
\{Q_{N,i}\}_{i=1}^{M_{N}^{n}}
satisfies (
5.28
). This suggests to consider a class
\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm good}}
of cubes in
\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2}
such that (
5.29
) holds: more precisely, we set
\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm good}}=(\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2})\setminus\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm bad}}
, where

\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm bad}}=\Big\{i\in\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2}:\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};J_{N,i})}{2\,\sigma_{N}^{n}}\geq 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})+\sqrt{\alpha_{N}}\Big\}\,,

is defined in dependence of the quantity

\displaystyle\alpha_{N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\max\Big\{\Big|\#(\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2})\,\sigma_{N}^{n}\,2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})-\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N},\mathbf{Q}_{1})}{2}\Big|\,,

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\Big[\Phi(\delta_{0})-\inf_{i\in\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2}}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N}^{\pm};J_{N,i}\cap G_{N}^{\pm})}{2\,\sigma_{N}^{n}}\Big]_{+}\Big\}\,,

(where
t_{+}:=\max\{t,0\}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
). We now claim that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\alpha_{N}=0\,,

(5.33)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\#\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm good}}}{M_{N}^{n}}=1\,,

(5.34)

and that
any choice
of
Q_{N}\in\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm good}}
satisfies (
5.28
) and (
5.29
).

To prove (
5.33
)
: By (
5.30
), (
5.31
) we have
\#(\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2})\,\sigma_{N}^{n}\to\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{n})=2^{n}
as
N\to\infty
, so that the first quantity in the definition of
\alpha_{N}
is vanishing as
N\to\infty
thanks to (
5.23
). We are thus left to prove that, if we set

L:=\liminf_{N\to\infty}\inf_{i\in\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2}}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N}^{\pm};J_{N,i}\cap G_{N}^{\pm})}{2\,\sigma_{N}^{n}}\,,

(5.35)

then
L\geq\Phi(\delta_{0})
. To prove this, let us consider
N_{k}\to\infty
,
i_{k}\in\mathcal{G}_{N_{k}}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N_{k}}^{2}
, and
m_{k}\in\{+,-\}
such that

L=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N_{k}}}(\tilde{u}_{N_{k}}^{m_{k}};J_{{N_{k}},{i_{k}}}\cap G_{N_{k}}^{m_{k}})}{2\,\sigma_{N_{k}}^{n}}\,.

Up to extracting a subsequence we can assume that either
m_{k}=+
or
m_{k}=-
for every
k
, and we can assume without loss of generality to be in the first case. Recalling that
Q_{N,i}=\xi_{N,i}+\mathbf{Q}_{\sigma_{N}/2}^{n}
and
J_{N,i}=Q_{N,i}\times(-1,1)
, if we set

J_{k}^{*}:=[(J_{N_{k},i_{k}}-\xi_{N_{k},i_{k}})/\sigma_{N_{k}}]=\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times\Big(-1\big/\sigma_{N_{k}},1\big/\sigma_{N_{k}}\Big)\,,

and define

G_{k}^{*}=[(G_{N_{k}}^{+}-\xi_{N_{k},i_{k}})/\sigma_{N_{k}}]\,,\qquad\mathbf{u}_{k}(z)=\tilde{u}_{N_{k}}^{+}(\sigma_{N_{k}}\,z+\xi_{N_{k},i_{k}})\,,\qquad z\in J_{k}^{*}\,,

then we have

\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N_{k}}}(\tilde{u}_{N_{k}}^{m_{k}};J_{{N_{k}},{i_{k}}}\cap G_{N_{k}}^{m_{k}})}{2\,\sigma_{N_{k}}^{n}}=\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{k};J_{k}^{*}\cap G_{k}^{*})}{2}\,.

(5.36)

Now, since
\tilde{u}_{N}^{+}\in W^{1,2}(\mathbf{Q}_{1})
implies
\mathbf{u}_{k}\in W^{1,2}(J_{k}^{*})
, and, by definition of
\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}
,

\displaystyle C(n)\,\sqrt{\beta_{N_{k}}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sigma_{N_{k}}^{n}}\,\int_{J_{N_{k},i_{k}}}|\tilde{u}_{N_{k}}^{+}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{-}}|=\sigma_{N_{k}}\,\int_{J_{k}^{*}}|\mathbf{u}_{k}-\delta_{0}1_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(-1/\sigma_{N_{k}},0)}|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}_{y}\,\fint_{-1/\sigma_{N_{k}}}^{1/\sigma_{N_{k}}}|\mathbf{u}_{k}(y,t)-\delta_{0}1_{(-1/\sigma_{N_{k}},0)}(t)|\,dt\,,

we find that, for
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-a.e.
y\in\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}
,
t\mapsto\mathbf{u}_{k}(y,t)
is absolutely continuous on
(-1/\sigma_{N_{k}},1/\sigma_{N_{k}})
and there are
a_{k}^{y}
and
b_{k}^{y}
in
(-1/\sigma_{N_{k}},1/\sigma_{N_{k}})
such that

\begin{split}&\lim_{k\to\infty}a_{k}^{y}=-\infty\,,\quad\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbf{u}_{k}(y,a_{k}^{y})=\delta_{0}\,,\\
&\lim_{k\to\infty}b_{k}^{y}=+\infty\,,\quad\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbf{u}_{k}(y,b_{k}^{y})=0\,;\end{split}

as a consequence, by (
MM
), by the fact that
\mathbf{u}_{k}
is constant on
J_{k}^{*}\setminus G_{k}^{*}
, by Fubini’s theorem, and by Fatou’s lemma, we find that

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{k};J_{k}^{*}\cap G_{k}^{*})}{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{J_{k}^{*}\cap G_{k}^{*}}|\partial_{x_{n+1}}(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{k})|=\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{J_{k}^{*}}|\partial_{x_{n+1}}(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{k})|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}}\liminf_{k\to\infty}\int_{(a_{k}^{y},b_{k}^{y})}|\partial_{x_{n+1}}(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{k})|\geq\Phi(\delta_{0})\,,

which, combined with (
5.36
), proves that
L\geq\Phi(\delta_{0})
, and thus that (
5.33
) holds.

To prove (
5.34
)
: We can estimate that

\frac{\#\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm bad}}}{M_{N}^{n}}\leq\frac{1}{M_{N}^{n}\,\sqrt{\alpha_{N}}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm bad}}}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};J_{N,i})}{\sigma_{N}^{n}}-2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})=A_{N}+B_{N}\,,

where, by definition of
\alpha_{N}
and since
(M_{N}\,\sigma_{N})^{n}\to\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{n})
as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle A_{N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{M_{N}^{n}\sqrt{\alpha_{N}}}\,\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2}}\Big\{\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};J_{N,i})}{\sigma_{N}^{n}}-2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};\mathbf{Q}_{1})-\#(\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2})\,\sigma_{N}^{n}\,2\Phi(\delta_{0})}{M_{N}^{n}\,\sigma_{N}^{n}\,\sqrt{\alpha_{N}}}\leq C(n)\,\sqrt{\alpha_{N}}\,,

and, again by definition of
\alpha_{N}
,

\displaystyle B_{N}:=-\frac{1}{M_{N}^{n}\sqrt{\alpha_{N}}}\,\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm good}}}\Big\{\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};J_{N,i})}{\sigma_{N}^{n}}-2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\Big\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{M_{N}^{n}\sqrt{\alpha_{N}}}\,\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm good}}}\Big\{\Phi(\delta_{0})-\sum_{m\in\{+,-\}}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};J_{N,i}\cap G_{N}^{m})}{2\,\sigma_{N}^{n}}\Big\}\leq C(n)\,\sqrt{\alpha_{N}}\,.

This shows that
\#\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm bad}}/M_{N}^{n}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
, and since
\#(\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2})/M_{N}^{n}\to 1
as
N\to\infty
by (
5.31
), we conclude the proof of (
5.34
).

To conclude the proof of (
5.28
) and (
5.29
)
: For an arbitrary choice of
i(N)\in\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm good}}
, let
Q_{N}:=Q_{N,i(N)}
and
J_{N}:=Q_{N}\times(-1,1)
. Since
\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm good}}\subset\mathcal{G}_{N}^{1}\cap\mathcal{G}_{N}^{2}
we deduce the validity of (
5.28
). At the same time, by
L\geq\Phi(\delta_{0})
, by
G_{N}^{+}\cap G_{N}^{-}=\varnothing
, by
\tilde{u}_{N}^{m}=\tilde{u}_{N}
on
G_{N}^{m}
for
m\in\{+,-\}
, and by the very definition of
\mathcal{G}_{N,{\rm good}}
, we see that

\displaystyle\Phi(\delta_{0})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\min_{m\in\{+,-\}}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}^{m}_{N};J_{N}\cap G_{N}^{m})}{2\,\sigma_{N}^{n}}\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m\in\{+,-\}}2\,\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}^{m}_{N};J_{N}\cap G_{N}^{m})}{2\,\sigma_{N}^{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};J_{N}\cap(G_{N}^{+}\cup G_{N}^{-}))}{2\,\sigma_{N}^{n}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\,\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{N}}(\tilde{u}_{N};J_{N})}{2\,\sigma_{N}^{n}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Phi(\delta_{0})+\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{\sqrt{\alpha_{N}}}{2}=\Phi(\delta_{0})\,,

which readily implies (
5.29
).

Step five (analysis of the second blow-up)
: With
Q_{N}=\xi_{N}+\mathbf{Q}_{\sigma_{N}/2}^{n}
and
J_{N}=Q_{N}\times(-1,1)
as in step five, if we now set

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(J_{N}-\xi_{N})/\sigma_{N}=\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(-1/\sigma_{N},1/\sigma_{N})\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{N}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}_{N}\cap\{x_{n+1}\gtrless 0\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}_{N}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[(J_{N}\cap G_{N}^{\pm})-\xi_{N}]/\sigma_{N}\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{N}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{N}(\xi_{N}+\sigma_{N}\,z)\,,

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{N}^{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{N}^{\pm}(\xi_{N}+\sigma_{N}\,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}_{N}(z)\,1_{\mathbf{G}_{N}^{\pm}}(z)+\delta_{N}\,1_{\mathbf{J}_{N}\setminus\mathbf{G}_{N}^{\pm}}(z)\,,

(where
m\in\{+,-\}
and
z\in\mathbf{J}_{N}
), then by (
5.28
), (
5.29
), (
5.21
), and (
5.22
), we find that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\max\Big\{\sigma_{N}\,\int_{\mathbf{J}_{N}}|\mathbf{u}_{N}^{+}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{J}_{N}^{-}}|\,,\sigma_{N}\,\int_{\mathbf{J}_{N}}|\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{J}_{N}^{+}}|\Big\}=0\,,

(5.37)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\max\Big\{\Big|2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})-\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N};\mathbf{J}_{N})}{2}\Big|,\Big|\Phi(\delta_{0})-\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N};\mathbf{G}_{N}^{\pm})}{2}\Big|\Big\}=0\,,

(5.38)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\mathbf{J}_{N}\cap\partial^{*}\mathbf{G}_{N}^{\pm}$ is $\mathcal{H}^{n}$-contained in $\{\mathbf{u}_{N}^{*}=\delta_{N}\}$}\,,

(5.39)

\displaystyle\mbox{$\mathbf{G}_{N}^{\pm}$ is $\mathcal{L}^{n+1}$-contained in $\{\mathbf{u}_{N}<\delta_{N}\}$}\,,

(5.40)

where
\sigma_{N}\to 0
and
\delta_{N}\to\delta_{0}
as
N\to\infty
. We now formulate a clam, where, for
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we use the notation,

\mathbf{T}_{a}(x)=x-a\,e_{n+1}\,,\qquad\mathbf{t}(x)=x_{n+1}\,,\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\,.

(5.41)

Claim
: there exists a sequence
\{a_{N}\}_{N}
with
\sigma_{N}\,|a_{N}|\leq 1/2
such that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z}|\mathbf{u}_{N}\circ\mathbf{T}_{a_{N}}-q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}|^{2}+|(\nabla\mathbf{u}_{N})\circ\mathbf{T}_{a_{N}}-(q_{0}^{\prime}\circ\mathbf{t})\,e_{n+1}|^{2}\,dx=0\,,

(5.42)

for every
Z\subset\subset(-\infty,Q_{0}]
. Here we denote by
q:\mathbb{R}\to(0,1)
the unique solution to

q^{\prime}=\sqrt{W(q)}\quad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}$}\,,\qquad q(0)=\frac{1}{4}\,,\qquad\lim_{t\to-\infty}q(t)=0\,,

(5.43)

and we define
q_{0}:\mathbb{R}\to(0,\delta_{0}]
as

\displaystyle q_{0}=1_{(-\infty,Q_{0}]}\,q+1_{(Q_{0},+\infty)}\,\delta_{0}\,,\qquad Q_{0}=q^{-1}(\delta_{0})\,.

(5.44)

(In particular,
Q_{0}>0
by
\delta_{0}\geq 1/2
, and
Q_{0}=+\infty
if and only if
\delta_{0}=1
.)

Conclusion of the theorem from the claim
: By (
5.26
), if we set
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{N}:=\lambda_{j_{N}}\,\varepsilon_{j_{N}}
, then

\int_{\mathbf{J}_{N}}{\rm ac}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N})\,{\rm div}\,Z-2\,\nabla\mathbf{u}_{N}\,\cdot\,\nabla Z[\nabla\mathbf{u}_{N}]=\mathbf{\Lambda}_{N}\,\int_{\mathbf{J}_{N}}V(\mathbf{u}_{N})\,{\rm div}\,Z\,,

for every
Z\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{J}_{N};\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
. In fact, setting
\mathbf{U}_{N}(x)=\mathbf{u}_{N}(x-a_{N}\,e_{n+1})
and noticing that by
\sigma_{N}\,|a_{N}|\leq 1/2
it holds that

B_{1}(z_{0})\subset\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(-1/2\,\sigma_{N},1/2\,\sigma_{N})\subset\mathbf{J}_{N}-a_{N}\,e_{n+1}

where
z_{0}=(Q_{0}-1)\,e_{n+1}
, we conclude that

\int_{B_{1}(z_{0})}{\rm ac}_{1}(\mathbf{U}_{N})\,{\rm div}\,Z-2\,\nabla\mathbf{U}_{N}\,\cdot\,\nabla Z[\nabla\mathbf{U}_{N}]=\mathbf{\Lambda}_{N}\,\int_{B_{1}(z_{0})}V(\mathbf{U}_{N})\,{\rm div}\,Z\,,

(5.45)

for every
Z\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{1}(z_{0});\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
. Let
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{1}(z_{0}))
be radially symmetric decreasing with respect to
z_{0}
, and set
Z(x)=\varphi(x)\,e_{n+1}
. In this way, denoting by
\rho
the reflection of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
with respect to
\{x_{n+1}=Q_{0}-1\}
and noticing that
\partial_{n+1}\varphi
is odd with respect to such reflection, we deduce by (
5.42
) that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{B_{1}(z_{0})}V(\mathbf{U}_{N})\,{\rm div}\,Z=\int_{B_{1}(z_{0})}V(q_{0}(x_{n+1}))\,\partial_{n+1}\varphi

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{1}(z_{0})\cap\{x_{n+1}>Q_{0}-1\}}\big\{V(q_{0}(x_{n+1}))-V\big(q_{0}(\rho(x)_{n+1})\big)\big\}\,\partial_{n+1}\varphi\,,

Now, since
q_{0}
is strictly increasing on
(-\infty,Q_{0}]
, and
V
is strictly increasing on
[0,1]
, we have
V(q_{0}(x_{n+1}))>V(q_{0}(\rho(x)_{n+1}))
for every
x\in B_{1}(z_{0})\cap\{x_{n+1}>Q_{0}-1\}
. Since
\varphi
being radially symmetric decreasing with respect to
z_{0}
implies that
\partial_{n+1}\varphi\leq 0
on
\{x_{n+1}>Q_{0}-1\}
, the choice of
\varphi
can thus be arranged so that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{B_{1}(z_{0})}V(\mathbf{U}_{N})\,{\rm div}\,Z<0\,.

(5.46)

At the same time, by (
5.42
) and
q_{0}^{\prime}=\sqrt{W(q_{0})}
we find that

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{B_{1}(z_{0})}{\rm ac}_{1}(\mathbf{U}_{N})\,{\rm div}\,Z-2\,\nabla\mathbf{U}_{N}\,\cdot\,\nabla Z[\nabla\mathbf{U}_{N}]

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{1}(z_{0})}\big[W(q_{0})-(q_{0}^{\prime})^{2}\big](x_{n+1})\,\partial_{n+1}\varphi=0\,,

which combined with (
5.45
) and (
5.46
) implies that
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{N}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
, and completes the proof of the theorem.

Proof of the claim
: We begin by reducing the proof of (
5.42
) to showing the existence of
\{b_{N}\}_{N}
and
\{c_{N}\}_{N}
with
\max\{|b_{N}|,|c_{N}|\}\,\sigma_{N}\leq 1/2
such that for every
W\subset\subset[P_{0},\infty)
and
Z\subset\subset(-\infty,Q_{0}]
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times W}|\mathbf{u}_{N}^{+}\circ\mathbf{T}_{b_{N}}-p_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}|^{2}+|(\nabla\mathbf{u}_{N}^{+}\circ\mathbf{T}_{b_{N}})-(p_{0}^{\prime}\circ\mathbf{t})\,e_{n+1}|^{2}\,dx=0\,,

(5.47)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z}|\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}}-q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}|^{2}+|(\nabla\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}})-(q_{0}^{\prime}\circ\mathbf{t})\,e_{n+1}|^{2}\,dx=0\,,

(5.48)

where we set denote by
p:\mathbb{R}\to(0,1)
the unique solution to

p^{\prime}=-\sqrt{W(p)}\quad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}$}\,,\qquad p(0)=\frac{1}{4}\,,\qquad\lim_{t\to+\infty}p(t)=0\,,

(5.49)

and we define
p_{0}:\mathbb{R}\to(0,\delta_{0}]
as

\displaystyle p_{0}=1_{[P_{0},+\infty)}\,p+1_{(-\infty,P_{0})}\,\delta_{0}\,,\qquad P_{0}=p^{-1}(\delta_{0})\,.

(5.50)

To deduce (
5.42
) from (
5.48
) and (
5.47
), we first note that for any
Z\subset\subset(-\infty,Q_{0}]
and
W\subset\subset[P_{0},\infty)
, since
Z\subset\{q_{0}<\delta_{0}\}
and
\{\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}<\delta_{N}\}=\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}
(which follows from the definition (
5.24
) of
\tilde{u}_{N}^{-}
), (
5.48
) and the analogous statements for
\mathbf{u}_{N}^{+}
imply that

\lim_{N\to\infty}|(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z)\setminus\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}}(\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-})|=0=\lim_{N\to\infty}|(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times W)\setminus\mathbf{T}_{b_{N}}(\mathbf{G}_{N}^{+})|\,.

(5.51)

As a consequence, the moving rectangles in
\mathbf{J}_{N}
on which
\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}
and
\mathbf{u}_{N}^{+}
are locally converging to translations of
q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}
and
p_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}
, respectively, cannot overlap too much: more precisely, given
C_{1},C_{2}>0
with
-2Q_{0}+C_{1}<C_{2}
, there exists
N_{0}(C_{1},C_{2})
such that

\displaystyle c_{N}-b_{N}\notin[-2Q_{0}+C_{1},C_{2}]\quad\forall N\geq N_{0}\,.

(5.52)

Indeed, if (
5.52
) did not hold for some
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
, then, up to a subsequence which we do not notate, we would have
c_{N}-b_{N}\to C^{\prime}\in[-2Q_{0}+C_{1},C_{2}]
. Then testing (
5.51
) with
W=[P_{0}+C_{1}/3,C_{2}]
and
Z=[-C_{2},Q_{0}-C_{1}/3]
would give

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}|(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times[P_{0}+C_{1}/3,C_{2}])\setminus\mathbf{T}_{b_{N}}(\mathbf{G}_{N}^{+})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}|(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times[b_{N}+P_{0}+C_{1}/3,b_{N}+C_{2}])\setminus\mathbf{G}_{N}^{+}|=\lim_{N\to\infty}|(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(b_{N}+W))\setminus\mathbf{G}_{N}^{+}|\,,

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}|(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times[-C_{2},Q_{0}-C_{1}/3])\setminus\mathbf{T}_{b_{N}}(\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}|(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times[c_{N}-C_{2},c_{N}+Q_{0}-C_{1}/3])\setminus\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}|=\lim_{N\to\infty}|(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(c_{N}+Z))\setminus\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}|\,.

Now since
b_{N}+W
and
c_{N}+Z
are intervals of equal length (by
P_{0}=-Q_{0}
), we may bound the length of their intersection from below by subtracting endpoints as follows:

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{L}^{1}\big((b_{N}+W)\cap(c_{N}+Z)\big)

\displaystyle\quad\geq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\min\{c_{N}+Q_{0}-C_{1}/3-(b_{N}+P_{0}+C_{1}/3),b_{N}+C_{2}-(c_{N}-C_{2})\}

\displaystyle\quad=\liminf_{N\to\infty}\min\{c_{N}-b_{N}+2Q_{0}-2C_{1}/3,b_{N}-c_{N}+2C_{2}\}

\displaystyle\quad=\min\{C^{\prime}-(-2Q_{0}+2C_{1}/3),2C_{2}-C^{\prime})\}\geq\min\{C_{1}/3,C_{2}\}>0\,.

But this contradicts
|\mathbf{G}_{N}^{+}\cap\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}|=0
, since the previous two estimates yield

\displaystyle 0=\lim_{N\to\infty}|\mathbf{G}_{N}^{+}\cap\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}|\geq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{H}^{n}(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n})\times\mathcal{L}^{1}\big((b_{N}+W)\cap(c_{N}+Z)\big)>0\,.

Moving on to proving (
5.42
), since
\mathbf{u}_{N}=\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}1_{\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}}+\mathbf{u}_{N}1_{\mathbf{J}_{N}\setminus\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}}
, (
5.51
) and (
5.48
) imply that for any
Z^{\prime}\subset\subset(-\infty,Q_{0}]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z^{\prime}}|\mathbf{u}_{N}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}}-q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}|^{2}dx=0\,.

(5.53)

To finish proving (
5.42
), we fix
Z\subset\subset(-\infty,Q_{0}]
. By (
5.53
), it suffices to show that, for
Z\subset\subset Z^{\prime}\subset\subset(-\infty,Q_{0}]
to be chosen shortly,

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z^{\prime}}|\nabla(\mathbf{u}_{N}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}})-q_{0}^{\prime}\circ\mathbf{t}\,e_{n+1}|^{2}dx=0\,.

(5.54)

We observe that as a consequence of the uniform energy bound (
5.38
), any subsequence
\{\mathbf{u}_{N_{k}}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N_{k}}}\}_{k}
of
\{\mathbf{u}_{N}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}}\}_{N}
has a further subsequence with a weak
W^{1,2}(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z^{\prime})
limit, which by (
5.53
), must be
q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}
. Therefore, the entire sequence
\{\mathbf{u}_{N}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}}\}_{N}
weakly converges in
W^{1,2}(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z^{\prime})
to
q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}
; in particular
\nabla(\mathbf{u}_{N}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}})\rightharpoonup q_{0}^{\prime}\circ\mathbf{t}\,e_{n+1}
in
L^{2}(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z^{\prime};\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
. To upgrade this weak convergence to (
5.54
), it is enough to show

\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z^{\prime}}|\nabla(\mathbf{u}_{N}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}})|^{2}dx=\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z^{\prime}}|q_{0}^{\prime}\circ\mathbf{t}\,e_{n+1}|^{2}dx\,.

(5.55)

Assuming for contradiction that (
5.55
) were false, then by the lower-semicontinuity of norms under weak convergence, we would have

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z^{\prime}}|\nabla(\mathbf{u}_{N}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}})|^{2}dx\geq\tau+\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z^{\prime}}|q_{0}^{\prime}\circ\mathbf{t}\,e_{n+1}|^{2}dx\,,\quad\tau>0\,.

(5.56)

Since
q
and
p
are optimal Allen-Cahn profiles, we may now choose
0<C_{1}<2Q_{0}
small enough and
C_{2}>0
large enough such that the set
Z^{\prime}=[-C_{2}/2,Q_{0}-C_{1}/2]\subset\subset(-\infty,Q_{0}]
compactly contains
Z
and such that
Z^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime}=[P_{0}+C_{1}/2,C_{2}/2]
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{1}(q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t};\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z^{\prime})/2+\mathcal{AC}_{1}(p_{0}\circ\mathbf{t};\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times W^{\prime})/2\geq 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})-\tau/2\,.

(5.57)

By (
5.52
), for each large
N
, either
c_{N}-b_{N}>C_{2}
or
b_{N}-c_{N}>2Q_{0}-C_{1}
. This implies that the intervals
c_{N}+Z^{\prime}
and
b_{N}+W^{\prime}
are disjoint for large
N
: indeed, two closed intervals
[\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]
and
[\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}]
are disjoint if and only if
\max\{\alpha_{3}-\alpha_{2},\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{4}\}>0
, and

\displaystyle\max\{c_{N}-C_{2}/2-(b_{N}+C_{2}/2),b_{N}+P_{0}+C_{1}/2-(c_{N}+Q_{0}-C_{1}/2)\}

\displaystyle\quad=\max\{c_{N}-b_{N}-C_{2},b_{N}-c_{N}-2Q_{0}+C_{1}\}>0\quad\textup{for large }N\,.

Then using in order (
5.38
) and the disjointness of
c_{N}+Z^{\prime}
and
b_{N}+W^{\prime}
; (
5.56
), the lower semicontinuity of norms under weak convergence, and Fatou’s lemma; and (
5.57
), we may compute

\displaystyle 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{AC}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N};\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}\times(c_{N}+Z^{\prime}))/2+\mathcal{AC}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N};\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}\times(b_{N}+W^{\prime}))/2

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\tau+\mathcal{AC}_{1}(q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t};\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}\times Z^{\prime})/2+\mathcal{AC}_{1}(p_{0}\circ\mathbf{t};\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}\times W^{\prime})/2

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})+\tau/2\,.

This is a contradiction since
\tau>0
. Thus (
5.55
) holds and gives (
5.42
).

The rest of the proof is thus devoted to showing the validity of (
5.48
); the proof of (
5.47
) is the same. To begin with, we show that, setting
\nabla^{\prime}=(\partial_{1},...,\partial_{n})
, we have

\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{J}_{N}}|\nabla^{\prime}\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}|^{2}=0\,,\qquad\limsup_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{J}_{N}}|\nabla(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})|\leq\Phi(\delta_{0})\,.

(5.58)

(In particular, local limits of
\mathbf{u}_{N}^{\pm}
will depend only on the
x_{n+1}
-variable.) Indeed, by (
5.37
), for
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-a.e.
