y\in\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}
we can find

\displaystyle s_{N}^{y}\in\Big(-\frac{1}{\sigma_{N}},-\frac{2}{3\,\sigma_{N}}\Big)\,,\quad\mbox{s.t.}\quad\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}(y,s_{N}^{y})=0\,,

(5.59)

\displaystyle t_{N}^{y}\in\Big(\frac{2}{3\,\sigma_{N}},\frac{1}{\sigma_{N}}\Big)\,,\quad\mbox{s.t.}\quad\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}(y,t_{N}^{y})=\delta_{0}\,.

Since, for
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-a.e.
y\in\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}
,

\mbox{$t\mapsto\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}(y,t)$ is absolutely continuous on $(-1/\sigma_{N},1/\sigma_{N})$ for every $N$}\,,

(5.60)

by Fubini’s theorem and Fatou’s lemma,

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{J}_{N}}|\partial_{n+1}(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})|\geq\int_{\mathbf{Q}^{n}_{1/2}}\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{(s_{N}^{y},t_{N}^{y})}|\partial_{n+1}(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})|\geq\Phi(\delta_{0})\,.

(5.61)

Combining (
5.61
) with (
5.38
) and with
\nabla(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})=0
a.e. on
\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}
, we find

\displaystyle\Phi(\delta_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{J}_{N}}|\partial_{n+1}(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})|=\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}}|\partial_{n+1}(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})|

(5.62)

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}}\big(|\partial_{n+1}\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}|^{2}+W(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})\big)/2\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}}\mathrm{ac}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})/2\leq\Phi(\delta_{0})\,.

from which we immediately deduce the first conclusion in (
5.58
); the limsup inequality in (
5.58
) is of course derived along similar lines.

Next, we claim that for every
\eta\in(0,\delta_{0})
, we can find sequences
\{c_{N}^{-}\}_{N}
and
\{c_{N}^{+}\}_{N}
with
-1/2\sigma_{N}\leq c_{N}^{-}<c_{N}^{+}<1/2\sigma_{N}
such that

\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(c_{N}^{-},c_{N}^{-}+1)}\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}=\eta\,,\qquad\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(c_{N}^{+},c_{N}^{+}+1)}\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}=\delta_{0}-\eta\,.

(5.63)

To prove this, let us consider the continuous function
f_{N}

defined by

f_{N}(t)=\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(t,t+1)}\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\,,\qquad|t|<\frac{1}{\sigma_{N}}-1\,.

Denoting by
k_{N}
the integer part of
1/(2\,\sigma_{N})
, we have that

\displaystyle\sigma_{N}\,\int_{\mathbf{J}_{N}}|\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}-\delta_{0}\,1_{\mathbf{J}_{N}^{+}}|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sigma_{N}\,\Big\{\sum_{k=0}^{k_{N}}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(k,k+1)}|\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}-\delta_{0}|+\sum_{h=0}^{k_{N}}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(-h-1,-h)}\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\Big\}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\sigma_{N}\,\Big\{\sum_{k=0}^{k_{N}}|f_{N}(k)-\delta_{0}|+\sum_{h=0}^{k_{N}}f_{N}(-h)\Big\}\,,

so that, by
\sigma_{N}\,k_{N}\geq(1/2)-\sigma_{N}\geq 1/4
(
N
large), we see that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\Big\{\inf_{0\leq k\leq k_{N}}|f_{N}(k)-\delta_{0}|+\inf_{0\leq h\leq k_{N}}f_{N}(-h)\Big\}=0\,.

In particular, for every
N
large enough depending on
\eta
, we can find integers
k,h\in(0,1/(2\sigma_{N}))
such that
f_{N}(k)>\delta_{0}-\eta
and
f_{N}(-h)<\eta
, and
c_{N}^{+}
and
c_{N}^{-}
satisfying (
5.63
) are then found by the intermediate value theorem.

By (
5.38
), both
\{\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{-}}\}_{N}
and
\{\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{+}}\}_{N}
are bounded in
W^{1,2}(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z)
for every
Z\subset\subset\mathbb{R}
. Thus, up to extracting a not relabeled subsequence and taking into account (
5.58
), we can find
q_{0}^{-},q_{0}^{+}\in(W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}\cap C^{0,1/2}_{\rm loc})(\mathbb{R};[0,\delta_{0}])
such that, as
N\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{split}&\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{-}}\rightharpoonup q_{0}^{-}\circ\mathbf{t}\,,\\
&\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{+}}\rightharpoonup q_{0}^{+}\circ\mathbf{t}\,,\qquad\mbox{weakly in $W^{1,2}(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z)$, $\forall\,Z\subset\subset\mathbb{R}$}\,,\end{split}\right.

(5.64)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{split}&\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{-}}\to q_{0}^{-}\circ\mathbf{t}\,,\\
&\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{+}}\to q_{0}^{+}\circ\mathbf{t}\,,\qquad\mbox{a.e. on $\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}$}\,.\end{split}\right.

(5.65)

In particular, by (
5.63
) and (
5.64
), we have that

\int_{(0,1)}q_{0}^{-}=\eta\,,\quad\int_{(0,1)}q_{0}^{+}=\delta_{0}-\eta\,,\quad\mbox{$\exists\,s_{*},t_{*}\in(0,1)$ s.t.}\left\{\begin{split}&q_{0}^{-}(s_{*})=\eta\,,\\
&q_{0}^{+}(t_{*})=\delta_{0}-\eta\,.\end{split}\right.

(5.66)

On combining (
5.66
), (
5.65
) and (
5.60
) we conclude that, for
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-a.e.
y\in\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}
,

\eta=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}(y,c_{N}^{-}+s_{*})\,,\qquad\delta_{0}-\eta=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}(y,c_{N}^{+}+t_{*})\,.

(5.67)

Since
(q_{0}^{\pm})^{\prime}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R})
, the limits

q_{0}^{\pm}(+\infty):=\lim_{t\to+\infty}q_{0}^{\pm}(t)\,,\qquad q_{0}^{\pm}(-\infty):=\lim_{t\to-\infty}q_{0}^{\pm}(t)\,,

exist, and we can prove that we always have

\{q_{0}^{+}(\pm\infty),q_{0}^{-}(\pm\infty)\}\subset\{0,\delta_{0}\}\,.

(5.68)

Indeed, by (
5.38
), by
\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}=\{\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}<\delta_{N}\}
and
\mathbf{u}_{N}=\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}
on
\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}
, by Fatou’s lemma, and by (
5.65
),

\displaystyle\Phi(\delta_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N};\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-})}{2}\geq\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}<\delta_{N}\}}W(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{-}}<\delta_{N}\}}W\big(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{-}}\big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\{\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{-}}<\delta_{N}\}}\liminf_{N\to\infty}W\big(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{-}}\big)\geq\int_{\{q_{0}^{-}<\delta_{0}\}}W(q_{0}^{-})\,,

and, similarly, we prove that
\int_{\{q_{0}^{+}<\delta_{0}\}}W(q_{0}^{+})<\infty
; since
W>0
on
(0,\delta_{0})
, we deduce (
5.68
). We now need to split the proof of (
5.48
) depending on the value of the limit inferior of
c_{N}^{+}-c_{N}^{-}\geq 0
.

Proof of (
5.48
) in the case when

\liminf_{N\to\infty}c_{N}^{+}-c_{N}^{-}\geq 1\,.

(5.69)

In this case we must have
c_{N}^{-}+s_{*}<c_{N}^{+}+t_{*}
for every
N
large enough. Therefore, by (
5.67
), we thus find

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times[c_{N}^{-}+s_{*},c_{N}^{+}+t_{*}]}|\nabla(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})|\geq\Phi(\delta_{0}-\eta)-\Phi(\eta)\,.

(5.70)

We can now improve (
5.68
) by showing that, if
\eta
is sufficiently small in terms of
\delta_{0}
and
W
, then

q_{0}^{+}(+\infty)=\delta_{0}\,,\qquad q_{0}^{-}(-\infty)=0\,.

(5.71)

Indeed, by using, in the order, (
5.66
) and the absolute continuity of
\Phi\circ q_{0}^{\pm}
, (
5.64
) and the lower semicontinuity of the total variation, the second conclusion in (
5.58
), and (
5.70
), we find

\displaystyle|\Phi(q_{0}^{-}(-\infty))-\Phi(\eta)|+|\Phi(q_{0}^{+}(+\infty))-\Phi(\delta_{0}-\eta)|\leq\int_{-\infty}^{s_{*}}|(\Phi\circ q_{0}^{-})^{\prime}|+\int_{t_{*}}^{\infty}|(\Phi\circ q_{0}^{+})^{\prime}|

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times[(-1/\sigma_{N},c_{N}^{-}+s_{*})\cup(c_{N}^{+}+t_{*},1/\sigma_{N})]}|D(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}^{-}_{N})|

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leq\Phi(\delta_{0})-\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(c_{N}^{-}+s_{*},c_{N}^{+}+t_{*})}|D(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}^{-}_{N})|

(5.72)

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\leq\Phi(\delta_{0})-\big(\Phi(\delta_{0}-\eta)-\Phi(\eta)\big)\,.

This inequality, combined with (
5.68
) and considered with
\eta
small enough in terms of
\delta_{0}
and
W
, implies (
5.71
).

Armed with (
5.71
), we prove that
q_{0}^{+}
is strictly increasing on
\{0<q_{0}^{+}<\delta_{0}\}
. Indeed, if this were not the case, then

\mbox{$\exists\,t_{1}<t_{2}$ s.t. $q_{0}^{+}(t_{1})\geq q_{0}^{+}(t_{2})\in(0,\delta_{0})$}\,.

(5.73)

Setting for brevity
I_{N}[t_{1},t_{2}]=\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times[t_{1}+c_{N}^{+},t_{2}+c_{N}^{+}]
we could then estimate

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-};\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-};\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}\setminus I_{N}[t_{1},t_{2}])+\mathcal{A}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-};\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}\cap I_{N}[t_{1},t_{2}])

(5.74)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2\,\int_{\mathbf{J}_{N}\setminus I_{N}[t_{1},t_{2}]}|D(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})|+\int_{\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}\cap I_{N}[t_{1},t_{2}]}W(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})\,.

Now, with
s_{N}^{y}
and
t_{N}^{y}
as in (
5.59
), we have

s_{N}^{y}<-\frac{2}{3\,\sigma_{N}}<-\frac{1}{2\sigma_{N}}<c_{N}^{-}<c_{N}^{+}<\frac{1}{2\,\sigma_{N}}<\frac{2}{3\,\sigma_{N}}<t_{N}^{y}\,,

so that, thanks to
\sigma_{N}\to 0^{+}
as
N\to\infty
, for
N
large enough we find
s_{N}^{y}<c_{N}^{+}+t_{1}
and
t_{N}^{y}>c_{N}^{+}+t_{2}

\mathcal{H}^{n}
-a.e. on
\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}
. In particular,

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{J}_{N}\setminus I_{N}[t_{1},t_{2}]}|D(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})|

\displaystyle\geq\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}_{y}\int_{c_{N}^{+}+t_{2}}^{t_{N}^{y}}|D(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})|+\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}}d\mathcal{H}^{n}_{y}\int_{s_{N}^{y}}^{c_{N}^{+}+t_{1}}|D(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})|

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}}\liminf_{N\to\infty}|\Phi(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}(y,t_{N}^{y}))-\Phi(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}(y,c_{N}^{+}+t_{2}))|

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}}\liminf_{N\to\infty}|\Phi(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}(y,c_{N}^{+}+t_{1}))-\Phi(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}(y,s_{N}^{y}))|\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}_{y}

\displaystyle=|\Phi(\delta_{0})-\Phi(q_{0}^{+}(t_{2}))|+|\Phi(q_{0}^{+}(t_{1}))-\Phi(0)|

\displaystyle=\Phi(\delta_{0})-\Phi(q_{0}^{+}(t_{2}))+\Phi(q_{0}^{+}(t_{1}))\geq\Phi(\delta_{0})\,,

(5.75)

where we have used
q_{0}^{+}(t_{2})\leq q_{0}^{+}(t_{1})
and the fact that
\Phi
is increasing on
[0,1]
. Concerning the second term in (
5.74
) we notice that, thanks to
\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}=\{\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}<\delta_{N}\}
we find

\int_{\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}\cap I_{N}[t_{1},t_{2}]}W(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})=\int_{\{\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{+}}<\delta_{N}\}\cap[\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(t_{1},t_{2})]}W(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}^{+}})\,,

so that

\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-}\cap I_{N}[t_{1},t_{2}]}W(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})\geq\int_{\{q_{0}^{+}<\delta_{0}\}\cap(t_{1},t_{2})}W(q_{0}^{+})=:c\,,

(5.76)

where
c>0
since
q_{0}^{+}(t_{1}),q_{0}^{+}(t_{2})\in(0,\delta_{0})
,
W>0
on
(0,\delta_{0})
, and
q_{0}^{+}
is continuous. By combining (
5.38
) with (
5.74
), (
5.75
) and (
5.76
) we conclude that

\Phi(\delta_{0})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathcal{A}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-};\mathbf{G}_{N}^{-})\geq\Phi(\delta_{0})+c>\Phi(\delta_{0})\,,

thus obtaining a contradiction with (
5.73
).

Having proved that
q_{0}^{+}
is strictly increasing on the (possibly unbounded) interval
(a,b)=\{0<q_{0}^{+}<\delta_{0}\}
, we see that it must be
q_{0}^{+}(t)\to 0^{+}
as
t\to a^{+}
. Since
q_{0}^{+}(+\infty)=\delta_{0}
implies
q_{0}^{+}(t)\to\delta_{0}
as
t\to b^{-}
, we find

\displaystyle\Phi(\delta_{0})=\int_{\{0<q_{0}^{+}<\delta_{0}\}}|(\Phi\circ q_{0}^{+})^{\prime}|\leq\int_{\{0<q_{0}^{+}<\delta_{0}\}}\mathcal{AC}_{1}(q_{0}^{+}\circ\mathbf{t})\leq\Phi(\delta_{0})\,,

so that
(q_{0}^{+})^{\prime}=\sqrt{W(q_{0})}
on
\{0<q_{0}^{+}<\delta_{0}\}
. By the Cauchy uniqueness theorem and by definition (
5.43
) of the Allen–Cahn one-dimensional profile
q
, since
q_{0}^{+}
is not identically equal to
0
, there exists
c_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

q_{0}^{+}(t)=q(t-c_{0})\,,\qquad\forall t\in\{0<q_{0}^{+}<\delta_{0}\}=\big(-\infty,c_{0}+Q_{0}\big)\,,

where, we recall,
Q_{0}:=q^{-1}(\delta_{0})
. Therefore, if we set
c_{N}=c_{N}^{+}+c_{0}
, then we conclude that

\mbox{$\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}}\rightharpoonup q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t}$ weakly in $W^{1,2}(\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times Z)$, $\forall\,Z\subset\subset\mathbb{R}$}\,,

(5.77)

where
q_{0}=1_{(-\infty,Q_{0}]}\,q+1_{(Q_{0},\infty)}\,\delta_{0}
is defined as in (
5.44
). The fact that

\displaystyle\Phi(\delta_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(-\infty,Q_{0})}|\nabla(q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t})|^{2}=\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{1}(q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t};\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(-\infty,Q_{0}))}{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}};\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(-\infty,Q_{0}))}{2}\leq\Phi(\delta_{0})\,,

implies that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(-\infty,Q_{0})}|\nabla(\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}\circ\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}})|^{2}=\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times(-\infty,Q_{0})}|\nabla(q_{0}\circ\mathbf{t})|^{2}\,,

and thus to improve the weak convergence stated in (
5.77
) to the strong convergence claimed in (
5.48
). This complete the proof of (
5.48
) in the case when (
5.69
) holds.

Proof of (
5.48
) in the case when

\liminf_{N\to\infty}c_{N}^{+}-c_{N}^{-}<1\,.

(5.78)

In this case, up to extracting a subsequence in
N
, we can assume that
c_{N}^{+}-c_{N}^{-}\to d_{0}\in[0,1)
. In particular, we find

q_{0}^{+}(t)=q_{0}^{-}(t-d_{0})\,,\qquad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}\,,

so that, by (
5.66
),

\int_{(-d_{0},-d_{0}+1)}q_{0}^{+}=\eta\,,\qquad\int_{(0,1)}q_{0}^{+}=\delta_{0}-\eta\,,

(5.79)

and, in particular,
d_{0}\in(0,1)
by taking
\eta<\delta_{0}/2
. With respect to the case when (
5.69
) holds, we now define
s_{*}
and
t_{*}
differently, by claiming that

\mbox{$\exists\,s_{*}<t_{*}$ s.t. $q_{0}^{+}(s_{*})=\eta$ and $q_{0}^{+}(t_{*})=\delta_{0}-2\,\eta$}\,.

(5.80)

Indeed, by (
5.79
) and the continuity of
q_{0}^{+}
, there is
s_{*}\in(-d_{0},-d_{0}+1)
such that
q_{0}^{+}(s_{*})=\eta
, while again by (
5.80
),

\int_{-d_{0}+1}^{1}q_{0}^{+}=\int_{0}^{1}q_{0}^{+}-\int_{0}^{-d_{0}+1}q_{0}^{+}=\delta_{0}-\eta-\int_{0}^{-d_{0}+1}q_{0}^{+}\geq\delta_{0}-2\,\eta\,,

so that there is also
r_{*}\in(-d_{0}+1,1)
(and thus, such that
r_{*}>s_{*}
) with the property that

q_{0}^{+}(r_{*})=\frac{1}{1-(-d_{0}+1)}\int_{-d_{0}+1}^{1}q_{0}^{+}\geq\frac{\delta_{0}-2\,\eta}{d_{0}}\geq\delta_{0}-2\,\eta\,.

Since
q_{0}^{+}(s_{*})=\eta
, we can find
t_{*}\in(s_{*},r_{*})
such that
q_{0}^{+}(t_{*})=\delta_{0}-2\,\eta
and conclude the proof of (
5.80
).

Having proved (
5.80
), we find

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{Q}_{1/2}^{n}\times[c_{N}^{+}+s_{*},c_{N}^{+}+t_{*}]}|\nabla(\Phi\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-})|\geq\Phi(\delta_{0}-2\,\eta)-\Phi(\eta)\,,

(5.81)

which we can use in place of (
5.70
) to argue as in (
5.72
) to prove (
5.71
) (that is,
q_{0}^{+}(+\infty)=\delta_{0}
and
q_{0}^{-}(-\infty)=0
). Without modifications one repeats the rest of the proof, from establishing that
q_{0}^{+}
is strictly increasing on
\{0<q_{0}^{+}\}
to showing that
q_{0}^{+}
is a translation of
q
and a strong
W^{1,2}
-limit of
\mathbf{T}_{c_{N}}\circ\mathbf{u}_{N}^{-}
for some finite translation
c_{N}
of
c_{N}^{+}
. The proof of the theorem is thus complete.
∎

6.
A hierarchy of Plateau problems (Proof of Theorem
1.2
)

This section is devoted the proof of Theorem
1.2
. We premise a simple lemma that will be used in the course of the proof.

Lemma 6.1
.

If
\mathbf{W}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
is compact,
\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus\mathbf{W}
,
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0^{+}
as
j\to\infty
,
u_{j}\in W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;[0,1])
,
u_{j}\to u
in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)
with
\sup_{j}\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j};\Omega)<\infty
,
\mathcal{V}(u_{j};\Omega)\to v>0
, and

\displaystyle{\rm ac}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j})\,\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\Omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptscriptstyle{*}}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\mu\,,\qquad\mbox{as Radon measures in $\Omega$}\,,

as
j\to\infty
, then

\liminf_{j\to\infty}\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j};\Omega)\geq\mu(\Omega)+\Theta(v,\varepsilon)\,,

where
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)
is the diffused Euclidean isoperimetric profile if
\varepsilon>0
(see Appendix
A
) and
\Theta(v,0)=(n+1)\,\omega_{n+1}^{1/(n+1)}\,v^{n/(n+1)}
.

Proof of Lemma
6.1
.

This can be proved by combining a localization argument which is quite common in the theory of concentration-compactness (and whose details are therefore omitted) with (
MM
) and the Euclidean isoperimetric inequality in its sharp and diffused versions.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

Momentarily assuming the validity of conclusion (i), we prove conclusions (ii), (iii), and (iv). To this end, let
\{u_{j}\}_{j}
be a sequence of minimizers for
\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
, where, after achieving
{\rm diam}\,\mathbf{W}=1
by scaling, we can assume that, as
j\to\infty
,

\displaystyle v_{j}\to v_{0}\in[0,\tau_{0}]\,,\qquad\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{v_{j}}\to 0^{+}\,,

(6.1)

\displaystyle\delta_{j}\to\delta_{0}\in\Big[\frac{1}{2},1\Big]\,,\qquad\min\{1-\delta_{j},v_{j}\}\to 0\,.

(6.2)

In this way, the situation of conclusion (ii) is met when
v_{0}>0
(in which case (
6.2
) forces
\delta_{0}=1
) and the situation of conclusion (iii) is met when
v_{0}=0
; and, in both cases, (
6.1
) implies that
\varepsilon_{j}\to 0^{+}
. Since
\ell<\infty
,
\Omega
has smooth boundary, and
\varepsilon_{j}/v_{j}\to 0^{+}
, we can apply Theorem
4.1
(if
v_{0}>0
) and Theorem
4.3
(if
v_{0}=0
), to find

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})\leq\begin{cases}\Psi_{\rm bk}(v_{0})\,,&\mbox{if $v_{0}>0$}\,,\\
2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\ell\,,&\mbox{if $v_{0}=0$}\,.\end{cases}

(6.3)

Therefore, by Theorem
3.4
, up to extracting subsequences, there exist
\mu
a Radon measure on
\Omega
and
(K,E)\in\mathcal{K}_{\rm B}
with
|E|\leq v_{0}
and
K\cup E^{{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}}
is
\mathcal{C}
-spanning, such that
\mu
is the weak-star limit of
\{|D(\Phi\circ u_{j})|\}_{j}
,
1_{E}
is
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)
-limit of
\{u_{j}\}_{j}
, and

\displaystyle\mu\geq\begin{cases}2\mathcal{H}^{n}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(K\cap E^{\scriptscriptstyle{(0)}})+\mathcal{H}^{n}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}(\partial^{*}E\cap\Omega)\,,&\mbox{if $v_{0}>0$}\,,\\
2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K\,,&\mbox{if $v_{0}=0$}\,.\end{cases}

(6.4)

We claim that
|E|=v_{0}
. If
v_{0}=0
this is trivial, while if
v_{0}>0
we can combine (
6.4
) with Lemma
6.1
to conclude that

\displaystyle\Psi_{\rm bk}(v_{0})\geq\mathcal{F}_{\rm bk}(K,E)+(n+1)\,\omega_{n+1}\,(v-|E|)^{n/(n+1)}\,.

In particular, if
|E|<v_{0}
, then we can construct a minimizing sequence for
\Psi_{\rm bk}(v_{0})
with positive volume loss at infinity, thus contradicting Theorem
1.1
-(iii). Having proved
|E|=v_{0}
, and thus that
(K,E)
is a minimizer of
\Psi_{\rm bk}(v_{0})
, we can combine (
6.3
), (
MM
) and (
6.4
) to find, in the case
v_{0}>0
,

\displaystyle\Psi_{\rm bk}(v_{0})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\limsup_{j\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j};\Omega)}{2}\geq\limsup_{j\to\infty}|D(\Phi\circ u_{j})|(\Omega)

(6.5)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\mu(\Omega)\geq\mathcal{F}_{\rm bk}(K,E)=\Psi_{\rm bk}(v_{0})\,;

and, in the case
v_{0}=0
,

2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\ell\geq\limsup_{j\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j};\Omega)}{2}\geq\limsup_{j\to\infty}|D(\Phi\circ u_{j})|(\Omega)\geq\mu(\Omega)\geq 2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\ell\,.

(6.6)

From (
6.5
) and (
6.6
) we find

\lim_{j\to\infty}\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})=\begin{cases}\Psi_{\rm bk}(v_{0})\,,&\mbox{if $v_{0}>0$}\,,\\
2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\ell\,,&\mbox{if $v_{0}=0$}\,,\end{cases}

thus proving conclusions (ii) and (iii), as well as, looking back at (
MM
),

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\Omega}\Big(\sqrt{\varepsilon_{j}}\,|\nabla u_{j}|-\sqrt{W(u_{j})/\varepsilon_{j}}\Big)^{2}=0\,,

from which conclusion (iv) follows immediately.

Proof of conclusion (i)
: Since the validity of the Euler–Lagrange equation in inner variation form is immediate from Lemma
4.5
-(i), it is really a matter of proving that for every positive
\tau_{0}
there is a positive
\tau_{1}
(depending on the data of the problem) such that
\Upsilon(v,\varepsilon,\delta)
admits a minimizer for every
(v,\varepsilon,\delta)\in{\rm SFR}(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
. We shall actually prove that for every such
(v,\varepsilon,\delta)
, every minimizing sequence of
\Upsilon(v,\varepsilon,\delta)
converge (modulo extracting subsequences) to a minimizer. To do this, after a rescaling that sets
{\rm diam}\,\mathbf{W}=1
, we argue by contradiction. This means assuming that there are a sequence

\{(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})\}_{j}
satisfying (
6.1
) and (
6.2
), and, for each
j
, a minimizing sequence
\{u_{j}^{k}\}_{k}
for
\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
, such that, up to extracting a diagonal subsequence, for each
j
there is
u_{j}^{0}\in W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}(\Omega;[0,1])
such that
u_{j}^{k}\to u_{j}^{0}
in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)
as
k\to\infty
,
but, for no index
j
,
u_{j}^{0}
is a minimizer of
\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
.

Now, by Theorem
3.2
and Theorem
3.4
, we know that

\mbox{$\{(u_{j}^{0})^{*}\geq t\}$ is $\mathcal{C}$-spanning $\mathbf{W}$ for every $t\in(1/2,\delta_{j})$}\,,

(6.7)

for every
j
, while by lower semicontinuity of the Allen–Cahn energy we have

\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})\geq\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)}{2}\,,\qquad\forall j\,.

(6.8)

Therefore the only possibility for
u_{j}^{0}
not to be a minimizer of
\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
is that

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}:=v_{j}-\mathcal{V}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)>0\,,\qquad\forall j\,.

(6.9)

We shall conclude the proof of the theorem by exploiting (
6.9
) to identify a subsequence
\{j(i)\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
such that, for every
i
large enough,

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j(i)}}(u^{k}_{j(i)})}{2}>\Upsilon(v_{j(i)},\varepsilon_{j(i)},\delta_{j(i)})\,,

(6.10)

thus obtaining the desired contradiction.

The idea behind the proof of (
6.10
) under (
6.9
) is to “bring back from infinity” the lost volume
\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}
in the form of a “half-bubble” that touches the wire frame, and then to exploit the fact that the isoperimetric profile of half-spaces is strictly less than the isoperimetric profile of
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
. There are several issues that must be addressed to make this approach work. First is the fact that we do not yet know that the sharp interface limit is a good approximation for the behavior of the escaping volume at infinity, since the sharp interface problem is, roughly speaking, closely describing the escaping volume only if
\varepsilon_{j}/(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{1/(n+1)}\to 0
. Second, unlike sharp interface problems, in which escaping volumes can be “brought back” and placed somewhere that does not disturb the rest of the configuration, Allen-Cahn minimizers will always have interacting tails. Without the regularity of minimizers (or even of limits of minimizing sequences), controlling these tail interactions will require care. Last but not least is the fact that the energy corresponding to a volume escaping at infinity corresponds to a lower order energy term whose presence does not contradict the leading order convergence of
\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
to, respectively,
\Psi_{\rm bk}(v_{0})
or to
2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\ell
. Any sort of analysis leading to the existence of minimizers must therefore be fine enough to detect vanishingly small inefficiencies in minimizing sequences with escaping volumes.

We divide the argument into steps. In step one, we sharply improve (
6.8
) to (
6.11
) and establish minimality and criticality properties of
u_{j}^{0}
. In step two, we conclude the contradiction in the case when
v_{0}>0
by using Theorem
1.1
-(iii). Step three deals with the case
v_{0}=0
, and it is in this case that the difficulties described in the previous paragraph are carefully addressed.

Step one
: Denoting by
\zeta_{j}
a radial minimizer with maximum at the origin for the diffused Euclidean isoperimetric problem
\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})
and by
\Lambda_{j}
the Lagrange multiplier of
\zeta_{j}
, so that
2\,\varepsilon_{j}^{2}\,\Delta\zeta_{j}=W^{\prime}(\zeta_{j})-\varepsilon_{j}\,\Lambda_{j}\,V^{\prime}(\zeta_{j})
on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
(see Appendix
A
), we claim that,

\displaystyle\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})=\big(\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)/2\big)+\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})\,,

(6.11)

\displaystyle\mbox{$u_{j}^{0}$ is a minimizer of $\Upsilon(v_{j}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})$}\,,

(6.12)

for every
j
, where
u_{j}^{0}
satisfies (
1.13
) with
\lambda=\Lambda_{j}
, that is

\int_{\Omega}\Big(\varepsilon_{j}\,|\nabla u_{j}^{0}|^{2}+\frac{W(u_{j}^{0})}{\varepsilon_{j}}\Big){\rm div}\,X-2\,\varepsilon_{j}\,\nabla u_{j}^{0}\,\cdot\,\nabla X[\nabla u_{j}^{0}]=\Lambda_{j}\,\int_{\Omega}V(u_{j}^{0})\,{\rm div}\,X\,,

(6.13)

whenever
X\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
with
X\cdot\nu_{\Omega}=0
on
\partial\Omega
.

Indeed, we can prove the
\leq
-part of (
6.11
) by constructing a competitor for
\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
obtained as a slight modification via Lemma
4.5
-(ii) of the
Ansatz

x\mapsto u_{j}^{0}(x)+\zeta_{j}(x-k\,e)
(
e\in\mathbb{S}^{n}
,
k
large). The matching lower bound is obtained by applying Lemma
6.1
to the minimizing sequence
\{u_{j}^{k}\}_{k}
of
\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
(notice that the lemma is applied here “at fixed
\varepsilon
”). Having proved (
6.11
), we notice that, again by the construction of Theorem
4.3
,

\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})\leq\Upsilon(v_{j}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})+\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})\,.

(6.14)

This inequality, combined with (
6.11
), implies (
6.12
). By (
6.12
), (
6.13
) holds indeed with some Lagrange multiplier
\lambda_{j}
: the fact that
\lambda_{j}=\Lambda_{j}
thus follows by a standard first variation argument (it they were different, we could violate (
6.11
)).

Step two
: We conclude the proof in the case when
v_{0}>0
. Indeed, up to extracting a further subsequence there is
\mathbf{v}^{\infty}_{0}\in[0,v_{0}]
such that
\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\to\mathbf{v}^{\infty}_{0}
as
j\to\infty
. Since
\mathcal{V}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)=v_{j}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\to v_{0}-\mathbf{v}_{0}^{\infty}
and (thanks to (
6.3
))
\sup_{j}\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)<\infty
, up to extracting subsequences we can apply Theorem
3.4
to conclude the existence of
(K,E)\in\mathcal{K}_{\rm B}
such that
u_{j}^{0}\to 1_{E}
in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)
,
K\cup E^{{\scriptscriptstyle{(1)}}}
is
\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
,
|E|\leq v_{0}-\mathbf{v}_{0}^{\infty}
, and
\liminf_{j}\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)/2\geq\mathcal{F}_{\rm B}(K,E)
(when exploiting (
3.19
), recall that in the present argument
v_{0}>0
implies
\delta_{j}\to 1^{-}
). We can actually improve on this lower bound by using Lemma
6.1
, thus concluding that

\displaystyle\liminf_{j\to\infty}\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)}{2}\geq\mathcal{F}_{\rm B}(K,E)+(n+1)\,\omega_{n+1}\,\big(v_{0}-\mathbf{v}_{0}^{\infty}-|E|\big)^{n/(n+1)}\,.

(6.15)

By (
6.3
), (
6.11
), (
6.15
), the continuity of
\Theta
(see Theorem
A.1
-(ii)), and the concavity of the Euclidean isoperimetric profile, we find that

\displaystyle\Psi_{\rm B}(v_{0})

\displaystyle\geq\limsup_{j\to\infty}\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})=\liminf_{j\to\infty}\big(\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)/2\big)+\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{F}_{\rm B}(K,E)+(n+1)\,\omega_{n+1}^{1/(n+1)}\,\Big\{(\mathbf{v}_{0}^{\infty})^{n/(n+1)}+\big(v_{0}-\mathbf{v}_{0}^{\infty}-|E|\big)^{n/(n+1)}\big\}

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{F}_{\rm B}(K,E)+(n+1)\,\omega_{n+1}^{1/(n+1)}\,2\,(v_{0}-|E|)^{n/(n+1)}\,.

(6.16)

Now, thanks to Theorem
1.1
-(iii), no minimizing sequence in
\Psi_{\rm B}(v_{0})
can lose volume at infinity. Therefore (
6.16
) implies that
|E|=v_{0}
and
\mathbf{v}_{0}^{\infty}=0
.

We can use the latter information to obtain a contradiction by arguing as follows. Since
|E|=v_{0}>0
implies that
1_{E}
is not constant in
\Omega
and
u_{j}^{0}\to 1_{E}
in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)
, by Lemma
4.5
-(ii) and
\mathcal{V}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)=v_{j}\to v_{0}
we can find diffeomorphisms
f_{j}:\Omega\to\Omega
such that
\mathcal{V}(u_{j}^{0}\circ f_{j};\Omega)=v_{j}
and

|\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0}\circ f_{j};\Omega)-\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)|\leq C_{0}\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)\,|\mathcal{V}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)-v_{j}|\leq C\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\,,

where
C_{0}
depends on
\Omega
and
E
as in Lemma
4.5
-(ii), and
C=C_{0}\,\sup_{j}\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)
. Since the homotopic spanning constraint is preserved under composition with a diffeomorphism of
\Omega
, we find that
u_{j}^{0}\circ f_{j}
is a competitor in
\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
, so that

\displaystyle 2\,\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})\leq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)+C\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\,;

(6.17)

at the same time, by (
6.11
), we have

\displaystyle 2\,\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})=\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)+2\,\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})\geq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)+2\,(n+1)\,\omega_{n+1}^{1/(n+1)}\,(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{n/(n+1)}\,

(6.18)

where the combination of (
6.17
) and (
6.18
) leads to a contradiction since
\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\to 0
.

Step three
: We are now left to consider the case when
v_{0}=0
. Thanks to
(
6.12
), (
6.11
),
v_{0}=0
, and (
6.1
),
\{u_{j}^{0}\}_{j}
is a sequence of minimizers for
\Upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
for some

\mathbf{v}_{j}:=v_{j}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\in(0,v_{j})

such that
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)\leq\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
,
v_{j}\to 0^{+}
, and
\varepsilon_{j}/v_{j}\to 0^{+}
. Therefore, by Theorem
5.1
and taking (
6.13
) into account, we have that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\varepsilon_{j}\,\Lambda_{j}=0\,,

(6.19)

as well as that (compare with (
5.6
))

2\,\Phi(\delta_{0})\,\mathcal{H}^{n}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}K={\rm w^{*}}\!\!\lim_{j\to\infty}|\nabla(\Phi\circ u_{j}^{0})|\,\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\Omega={\rm w^{*}}\!\!\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{{\rm ac}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0})}{2}\,\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\mathbin{\vrule height=6.88889pt,depth=0.0pt,width=0.55974pt\vrule height=0.55974pt,depth=0.0pt,width=5.59721pt}\Omega\,,

(6.20)

where
K
is a minimizer of
\ell
.

Recalling that
\zeta_{j}
is a minimizer in
\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})
and that
\Lambda_{j}=\Lambda(\zeta_{j})
, by (
A.4
) (see Theorem
A.1
-(iii)) we have that

\Lambda_{j}\geq\frac{c(n,W)}{(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{1/(n+1)}}\,.

(6.21)

This inequality, combined with (
6.19
), gives in particular

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{\varepsilon_{j}}{(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{1/(n+1)}}=0\,.

(6.22)

Hence, for
j
large enough, we have
\varepsilon_{j}<\sigma_{0}\,(2\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{1/(n+1)}
for
\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{0}(n,W)>0
as in Theorem
A.1
-(iv). Setting
\sigma_{j}=\varepsilon_{j}/(2\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{1/(n+1)}
, and recalling that
\zeta_{v,\varepsilon}
denotes the unique modulo translations radially symmetric decreasing minimizer of
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)
when
\varepsilon<\sigma_{0}\,v^{1/(n+1)}
, let us now consider that

\zeta_{2\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j}}(x)=\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}\Big(x\big/(2\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{1/(n+1)}\Big)\,,\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\,,

where we have set
\pi_{j}(x)=x/(2\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{1/(n+1)}
(
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
); denoting by
B_{r(n)}
the unit volume ball with center at the origin, the fact that
\sigma_{j}\to 0^{+}
guarantees that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}|\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}-1_{B_{r(n)}}|=0\,,\qquad\gamma_{j}:=\max\Big\{\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}};B_{2\,r(n)}^{c}),\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{j}}(\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}};B_{2\,r(n)}^{c})\Big\}\to 0\,.

Hence, by Lemma
4.5
, there exist
\eta_{0}>0
such that for every
j
large enough and every
|\eta|<\eta_{0}
there is a radially symmetric diffeomorphism
f_{j}^{\eta}:B_{2\,r(n)}\to B_{2\,r(n)}
with
\{f_{j}^{\eta}\neq{\rm id}\,\}\subset\subset B_{2\,r(n)}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}\circ f_{j}^{\eta})=\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}})+\eta=1+\eta\,,

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{j}}(\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}\circ f_{j}^{\eta})-\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{j}}(\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}})\big|\leq C(n)\,\Theta(1,\sigma_{j})\,|\eta|\,.

By radial symmetry of
\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}\circ f_{j}^{\eta}
and
\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}
, if we set
H=\{x_{n+1}>0\}
, then, for every
|\eta|<\eta_{0}
and
j
large enough,

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}\circ f_{j}^{\eta};H)=\frac{1}{2}+\eta\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{j}}(\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}\circ f_{j}^{\eta};H)\leq\big(1+C(n)\,|\eta|\big)\,\Theta(1,\sigma_{j})\,.

Then the functions
\zeta_{j}^{\eta}=\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}\circ f_{j}^{\eta}\circ\pi_{j}
satisfy

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta};H)=2\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\,\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}\circ f_{j}^{\eta};H)=\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}+\eta\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta};H)=(2\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{n/(n+1)}\,\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta};H)\leq\big(1+C(n)\,|\eta|\big)\,\Theta(2\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})\,.

At the same time, by (
6.22
) we can use (
A.5
) to find

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{\Theta(2\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})}{\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})}=2^{n/(n+1)}\,,

(6.23)

so that there are
\beta(n)\in(0,1)
and
J_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\frac{\Theta(2\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})}{2}\leq(1-\beta(n))\,\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})\,,\qquad\forall j\geq J_{0}\,,

and, in summary,

\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta};H)}{2}\leq\big(1-\beta(n)+C(n)|\eta|\big)\,\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})\,,\qquad\forall j\geq j_{0}\,,|\eta|<\eta_{0}\,.

(6.24)

We next notice that, by the smoothness of
\partial\mathbf{W}
and up to a rigid motion that takes
0\in\partial\mathbf{W}
and
\nu_{\mathbf{W}}(0)=e_{n+1}
, we can find positive constant
C
and
r^{\prime}
depending on
\mathbf{W}
such that

\mathcal{H}^{n}((H\Delta\Omega)\cap\partial B_{r})\leq C\,r^{n+1}\,,\qquad\forall r<r^{\prime}\,,

(6.25)

where
x=(x^{\prime},x_{n+1})\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, if we set for brevity

r_{j}:=(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{1/(n+1)}\,2\,r(n)

them,, up to increase the value of
J_{0}
so to have
r_{j}<r^{\prime}
when
j\geq J_{0}
, and noticing that
\mathrm{ac}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta})
is a radial function, by
\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}=\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}\circ f_{j}^{\eta}
on
B_{2\,r(n)}^{c}
and by definition of
\gamma_{j}
,

\displaystyle\Big|\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta};\Omega)-\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta};H)\Big|\leq\int_{(\Omega\Delta H)\cap B_{r_{j}}}\mathrm{ac}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta})+2\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta};B_{r_{j}}^{c})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{r_{j}}\,\mathcal{H}^{n}((\Omega\Delta H)\cap\partial B_{r})\,\mathrm{ac}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta})\,dr+2\,(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{n/(n+1)}\,\mathcal{AC}_{\sigma_{j}}(\zeta_{1,\sigma_{j}}\circ f_{j}^{\eta};B_{2\,r(n)}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{0<r<r_{j}}\Big\{\frac{\mathcal{H}^{n}((\Omega\Delta H)\cap\partial B_{r})}{\mathcal{H}^{n}(H\cap\partial B_{r})}\Big\}\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta};H)+2\,(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{n/(n+1)}\,\gamma_{j}

\displaystyle\leq C(n,\mathbf{W})\,r_{j}\,\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})+2\,(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{n/(n+1)}\,\gamma_{j}={\rm o}_{n,W}\big(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\big)^{n/(n+1)}\,.

where in the last two inequalities we have used, in the order, by (
6.25
), (
6.24
), (
A.5
), and
\gamma_{j}\to 0
. By combining this inequality with (
6.24
) we thus conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta};\Omega)}{2}\leq\Big(1-\beta(n)+C(n)\,|\eta|\Big)\,\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})+{\rm o}_{n,W}\big(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\big)^{n/(n+1)}\,,

(6.26)

(where we recall that
\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})/(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{n/(n+1)}\to c(n)>0
as
j\to\infty
by (
A.5
).) By an identical argument we also find that

\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{j}^{\eta};\Omega)=\big(1+\eta\big)\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}+{\rm o}_{n,W}\big(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\big)\,.

In summary, we can claim the existence of
J_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and
\eta_{0}>0
such that for each
x\in\partial\mathbf{W}
we can find
\zeta_{j}^{x,\eta}
with the properties that

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{j}^{x,\eta};B_{r_{j}}(x)^{c})={\rm o}_{n,W}\big(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\big)\,,\qquad\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta_{j}^{x,\eta};B_{r_{j}}(x)^{c})={\rm o}_{n,W}\big(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\big)^{n/(n+1)}\,,\hskip 28.45274pt

(6.27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{j}^{x,\eta};\Omega)=\big(1+\eta\big)\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}+{\rm o}_{n,W}\big(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\big)

(6.28)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{x,\eta};\Omega)}{2}\leq\Big(1-\beta(n)+C(n)\,|\eta|\Big)\,\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})+{\rm o}_{n,W}\big(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\big)^{n/(n+1)}\,,

(6.29)

where
r_{j}=(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{1/(n+1)}\,2\,r(n)
.

The final step in the construction is making a choice of
x=x_{j}\in\partial\mathbf{W}
such that the interaction between
\zeta_{j}^{x,\eta}
and
u_{0}^{j}
in minimized. We claim that indeed
x_{j}\in\partial\mathbf{W}
can be found such that

\mathcal{V}(u_{0}^{j};\Omega\cap B_{r_{j}}(x_{j}))={\rm o}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})\,.

(6.30)

We now show, first, how to derive a contradiction from (
6.27
), (
6.28
), (
6.29
), and (
6.30
); and, finally, how to prove (
6.30
). In this way the proof of the theorem will be complete.

Derivation of a contradiction from (
6.27
), (
6.28
), (
6.29
), and (
6.30
)
: Let us consider the functions

h_{j}^{\eta}=\max\big\{u_{0}^{j},\zeta_{j}^{x_{j},\eta}\big\}\,,\qquad j\geq J_{0}\,,|\eta|<\eta_{0}\,.

Since
h_{j}^{\eta}\geq u_{0}^{j}
on
\Omega
, we have that
\{(h_{j}^{\eta})^{*}\geq t\}
is
\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
for every
t\in[1/2,\delta_{j})
. We claim that we can find
|\eta_{j}|<\eta_{0}
such that

\mathcal{V}(h_{j}^{\eta_{j}};\Omega)=v_{j}\,,\qquad\lim_{j\to\infty}\eta_{j}=0\,.

(6.31)

We start noticing that by (
6.27
), (
6.30
),
V(u_{0}^{j})\leq V(\zeta_{j}^{x_{j},\eta})
on
\{u_{0}^{j}\leq\zeta_{j}^{x_{j},\eta}\}
, and
V(u_{0}^{j})\geq V(\zeta_{j}^{x_{j},\eta})
on
\{u_{0}^{j}\geq\zeta_{j}^{x_{j},\eta}\}
, we find that

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(h_{j}^{\eta};\Omega)=\mathcal{V}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)+\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{j}^{x_{j},\eta};\Omega)+{\rm o}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})\,.

Therefore, by (
6.28
), and recalling that, by definition
\mathcal{V}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)=v_{j}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}
, we find that

\mathcal{V}(h_{j}^{\eta};\Omega)=v_{j}+\eta\,\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}+{\rm o}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})\,.

(6.32)

In particular, up to increase the value of
J_{0}
, if
j\geq J_{0}
we have
\mathcal{V}(h_{j}^{\eta_{0}/2};\Omega)>v_{j}
and
\mathcal{V}(h_{j}^{-\eta_{0}/2};\Omega)<v_{j}
. By continuity of
\big(|\eta|\leq\eta_{0}/2\big)\mapsto\mathcal{V}(h_{j}^{\eta};\Omega)
we find
|\eta_{j}|\leq\eta_{0}/2
such that
\mathcal{V}(h_{j}^{\eta_{j}};\Omega)=v_{j}
. Plugging this information back into (
6.32
) we find that
\eta_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
.

To derive a contradiction we notice that, being
h_{j}^{\eta_{j}}
admissible in
\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})
, by (
6.11
) and (
6.29
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)+2\,\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})=2\,\Upsilon(v_{j},\varepsilon_{j},\delta_{j})\leq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}\big(h_{j}^{\eta_{j}};\Omega\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}\big(u_{j}^{0};\Omega\cap\{u_{j}^{0}\geq\zeta_{j}^{x_{j},\eta_{j}}\}\big)+\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}\big(\zeta_{j}^{x_{j},\eta_{j}};\Omega\cap\{\zeta_{j}^{x_{j},\eta_{j}}\geq u_{0}^{j}\}\big)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)+\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(\zeta_{j}^{x_{j},\eta_{j}};\Omega)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};\Omega)+2\,\Big(1-\beta(n)+C(n)\,|\eta_{j}|\Big)\,\Theta(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty},\varepsilon_{j})+{\rm o}_{n,W}\big(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\big)^{n/(n+1)}\,,

which leads to a contradiction with
\beta(n)\in(0,1)
as soon as
j
is large enough.

Proof of (
6.30
):
We finally prove the existence of
x_{j}\in\partial\mathbf{W}
such that (
6.30
) holds. To this end, we recall the validity of (
6.20
), where
K
is a minimizer of
\ell
. By exploiting this minimality property as done in
[
KMS22a
, Proof of Theorem 1.4, Step 6]
, we see that
K
does not concentrate area near
\partial\mathbf{W}
, that is

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\big(K\cap\{x:{\rm dist}(x,\partial\mathbf{W})<r\}\big)\leq C\,r\,,\qquad\forall r>0\,,

(6.33)

with some
C
depending on
K
. Moreover, as shown for example in
[
KMS22a
, Appendix B]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}(K\cap B_{r}(x))\geq c_{0}r^{n}\quad\forall x\in\mathrm{cl}\,(K)\,,\,\,r\in(0,r_{0})\,.

(6.34)

By combining (
6.33
) and (
6.34
) we see that
K
is not “wetting” the whole
\partial\mathbf{W}
, that is

(\partial\mathbf{W})\setminus\mathrm{cl}\,(K)\neq\varnothing\,.

(6.35)

In particular, there are
x_{0}\in\partial\mathbf{W}
and
r_{0}>0
such that
\mathrm{cl}\,B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})\cap K=\varnothing
, so that, by (
6.20
), and taking also into account that
u_{j}^{0}\to 0
in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)
, we find

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})\cap\Omega)=0\,.

(6.36)

Correspondingly to
x_{0}
, and up to decreasing the value of
r_{0}
, we can find a cube
Q\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
and an embedding
g
of
Q\times[0,r_{0})
into
\overline{\Omega}\cap B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})
so that
g
embeds
Q\times\{0\}
into
(\partial\Omega)\cap B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})
and
g
is arbitrarily
C^{1}
-close to an isometry, in such a way that if
Q^{\prime}
is a cube contained in
Q
with side length
2\,s
and center
z
, then

\Omega\cap B_{s}(g(z))\subset g(Q^{\prime}\times(0,s))\,.

(6.37)

If
j
is large enough, then we can find a partition
\mathcal{F}_{j}=\{Q_{j}^{k}\}_{k=1}^{N(j)}
of
Q
into subcubes of sidelength
s_{j}>r_{j}
for some
s_{j}
and
N_{j}
satisfying

s_{j}={\rm O}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{1/(n+1)}\,,\qquad N_{j}={\rm O}(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty})^{-n/(n+1)}\,.

(6.38)

The subfamily
\mathcal{G}_{j}
defined by

\mathcal{G}_{j}=\Big\{Q_{j}^{k}:\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}\big(u_{0}^{j};g(Q_{j}^{k}\times(0,s_{j}))\big)\leq\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})\cap\Omega)^{1/2}}{N(j)}\Big\}

is of course such that

0\leq 1-\frac{\#\,\mathcal{G}_{j}}{N(j)}\leq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}(u_{j}^{0};B_{r_{0}}(x_{0})\cap\Omega)^{1/2}\,,

(6.39)

and by (
6.36
) and (
6.38
) we have

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon_{j}}\big(u_{0}^{j};g(Q_{j}^{k}\times(0,s_{j}))\big)={\rm o}\big(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\big)^{n/(n+1)}\,,\qquad\forall Q_{j}^{k}\in\mathcal{G}_{j}\,.

(6.40)

We now claim that, for every
j
large enough, there is
Q_{j}^{k(j)}\in\mathcal{G}_{j}
such that

\mathcal{V}(u_{0}^{j};g(Q_{j}^{k(j)}\times(0,s_{j}))\big)={\rm o}\big(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\big)

(6.41)

Denoting by
z_{j}
the center of
Q_{j}^{k(j)}
and setting
x_{j}=g(z_{j})
, and by applying (
6.37
) with
s=s_{j}>r_{j}
, we conclude that

\mathcal{V}(u_{0}^{j};\Omega\cap B_{r_{j}}(x_{j}))\leq\mathcal{V}\big(u_{0}^{j};g(Q_{j}^{k(j)}\times(0,s_{j}))\big)={\rm o}\big(\mathbf{v}_{j}^{\infty}\big)\,,

and complete the proof of (
6.30
). To prove (
6.41
), we argue by contradiction. Should (
6.41
) fail, then, up to extracting a subsequence in
j
and for some constant
c_{0}>0
, we would have that

\mathcal{V}(u_{0}^{j};g(Q_{j}^{k}\times(0,s_{j}))\big)\geq c_{0}\,s_{j}^{n+1}\,,\qquad\forall Q_{j}^{k}\in\mathcal{G}_{j}\,,

(6.42)

so that, for some
c_{1}\in(0,1)
,

c_{1}\leq\fint_{Q_{j}^{k}\times(0,s_{j})}V(u_{0}^{j}\circ g)\,,\qquad\forall Q_{j}^{k}\in\mathcal{G}_{j}\,.

Since
V(u_{0}^{j}\circ g)
takes values in
[0,1]
we find that

\displaystyle\frac{c_{1}}{2}\,|Q_{j}^{k}\times(0,s_{j})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big|\Big\{(y,s)\in Q_{j}^{k}\times(0,s_{j}):V(u_{0}^{j}\circ g)\geq\frac{c_{1}}{2}\Big\}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{n}\Big(\Big\{y\in Q_{j}^{k}:\sup_{(0,s_{j})}\,V((u_{0}^{j})^{*}(g(y,\cdot)))\geq\frac{c_{1}}{2}\Big\}\Big)\,s_{j}\,,

that is, for some
c\in(0,1)
and recalling that
V
is strictly increasing on
(0,1)
,

c\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(Q_{j}^{k})\leq\mathcal{H}^{n}\Big(\Big\{y\in Q_{j}^{k}:\sup_{(0,s_{j})}\,(u_{0}^{j})^{*}(g(y,\cdot))\geq c\Big\}\Big)\,,\qquad\forall Q_{j}^{k}\in\mathcal{G}_{j}\,.

Adding up over all the cubes in
\mathcal{G}_{j}
, and recalling (
6.39
) and that
\mathcal{F}_{j}
is a partition of
Q
, we thus conclude (up to further decrease the value of
c
) that

\mathcal{H}^{n}\Big(\Big\{y\in Q:\sup_{(0,s_{j})}\,(u_{0}^{j})^{*}(g(y,\cdot))\geq c\Big\}\Big)\geq c\,\mathcal{H}^{n}(Q)\,,\qquad\forall j\,.

(6.43)

However, by (
6.36
), (
MM
), Fubini’s theorem, the area formula, and the slicing theory for Sobolev functions (see, e.g.
[
EG92
, Section 4.9.2]
),
there is a set
Z\subset Q
with full
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-measure in
Q
such that, for every
y\in Z
,
(u_{0}^{j})^{*}(g(y,\cdot))
is absolutely continuous on
(0,r_{0})
,
(u_{0}^{j})^{*}(g(y,\cdot))\to 0

\mathcal{H}^{1}
-a.e. on
(0,r_{0})
as
j\to\infty
(recall indeed that
\mathcal{V}(u_{0}^{j};\Omega)\to 0
), and

2\,\int_{g(\{y\}\times(0,r_{0}))}|\nabla[\Phi\circ(u_{0}^{j})^{*}]|\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}\leq\int_{g(\{y\}\times(0,r_{0}))}\varepsilon_{j}|\nabla(u_{0}^{j})^{*}|^{2}+\frac{W((u_{0}^{j})^{*})}{\varepsilon_{j}}\,d\mathcal{H}^{1}\to 0\,.

In particular, we find that

\lim_{j\to\infty}\sup_{(0,r_{0})}(u_{0}^{j})^{*}(g(y,\cdot))=0\,,\qquad\forall y\in Z\,,

and obtain a contradiction with (
6.43
).
∎

7.
Euler-Lagrange equation and regularity (Proof of Theorem
1.3
)

We finally prove Theorem
1.3
and Proposition
1.4
.

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

By Theorem
1.2
.(i), there is
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\int_{\Omega}\Big(\varepsilon|\nabla u|^{2}+\frac{W(u)}{\varepsilon}\Big){\rm div}\,X-2\,\varepsilon\nabla u\,\cdot\,\nabla X[\nabla u]=\lambda\,\int_{\Omega}V(u)\,{\rm div}\,X\,,

(7.1)

whenever
X\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
; also, (
7.1
) extends to
X\in C_{c}^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
by density.

Step one
: We claim that if
\varphi\in C_{c}^{1}(\Omega)
and
h\in{\rm Lip}_{c}([0,1))
with
\varphi\geq 0
and
h\geq 0
, then

0\leq\int_{\Omega}\varphi\,h^{\prime}(u)\,|\nabla u|^{2}+h(u)\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi+\varphi\,h(u)\,F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)\,,

(7.2)

where

F_{\varepsilon}(t)=\frac{1}{2\,\varepsilon}\,\Big\{\frac{W(t)}{\varepsilon}-\lambda\,V(t)\Big\}\,,\qquad t\in[0,1]\,.

To prove this, we start by noticing that there is
\sigma_{0}>0
small enough (depending on
h
and
\varphi
) such that, if
\sigma\in[0,\sigma_{0})
, then

\mbox{$u_{\sigma}=u+\sigma\,h(u)\,\varphi$ takes values in $[0,1]$}\,.

Since
u_{\sigma}\geq u
implies that
\{u_{\sigma}^{*}\geq t\}
is
\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
for every
t\in(1/2,\delta)
, in order to make
u_{\sigma}
admissible in
\Upsilon(v,\varepsilon,\delta)
we just need to compose with a diffeomorphism in order to restore the volume constraint. To this end, given
X\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
with

\int_{\Omega}V(u)\,{\rm div}\,\,X=1\,,

(7.3)

let
\tau_{0}>0
be small enough so that, defining
\Phi\in C^{\infty}((-\tau_{0},\tau_{0})\times\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
by
\Phi(\tau,x)=\Phi_{\tau}(x)=x+\tau\,X(x)
, we have that
\Phi_{\tau}
is a diffeomorphism of
\Omega
for every
|\tau|<\tau_{0}
. Denoting by
\Psi_{\tau}
the inverse of
\Phi_{\tau}
, and letting
g(\sigma,\tau)=\mathcal{V}\big(u_{\sigma}\circ\Psi_{\tau}\big)
, we observe that
g(0,0)=v
with
\partial_{\tau}g(0,0)=\int_{\Omega}V(u)\,{\rm div}\,X=1
by (
7.3
). Therefore, by the implicit function theorem, up to decreasing the value of
\sigma_{0}
, we can find
m(\sigma)
with
m(0)=0
,
|m(\sigma)|<\tau_{0}
, and
g(\sigma,m(\sigma))=v
for every
\sigma\in[0,\sigma_{0})
– in particular, differentiating
g(\sigma,m(\sigma))=v
and recalling (
7.3
), we find

0=m^{\prime}(0)+\int_{\Omega}h(u)\,\varphi\,V^{\prime}(u)\,.

(7.4)

Since
v_{\sigma}=u_{\sigma}\circ\Psi_{m(\sigma)}
is admissible in
\Upsilon(v,\varepsilon,\delta)
for every
\sigma\in[0,\sigma_{0})
, the minimality of
u=u_{\sigma=0}
in
\Upsilon(v,\varepsilon,\delta)
implies that
f(\sigma)=\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{\sigma}\circ\Psi_{m(\sigma)})
has a minimum on
[0,\sigma_{0})
at
\sigma=0
. By combining
f^{\prime}(0)\geq 0
with (
7.4
), (
7.1
) and (
7.3
) we thus find

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle m^{\prime}(0)\,\int_{\Omega}\Big\{\varepsilon\,|\nabla u|^{2}+\frac{W(u)}{\varepsilon}\Big\}\,{\rm div}\,X-2\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla X[\nabla u]

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}2\,\varepsilon\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla[h(u)\,\varphi]+\frac{W^{\prime}(u)}{\varepsilon}\,h(u)\,\varphi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\lambda\,\int_{\Omega}h(u)\,\varphi\,V^{\prime}(u)+\int_{\Omega}2\,\varepsilon\,\varphi\,h^{\prime}(u)|\nabla u|^{2}+2\,\varepsilon\,h(u)\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi+\frac{W^{\prime}(u)}{\varepsilon}\,h(u)\,\varphi\,,

that is (
7.2
) by definition of
F_{\varepsilon}(t)
.

Step two
: We prove that

2\,\varepsilon^{2}\,\Delta u\leq W^{\prime}(u)-\lambda\,\varepsilon\,V^{\prime}(u)\quad\mbox{as distributions on $\Omega$}\,.

(7.5)

Indeed, let
\{h_{k}\}_{k}\subset C^{1}_{c}([0,1))
be a sequence such that
0\leq h_{k}(t)\leq h_{k+1}(t)\to 1
for every
t\in[0,1)
and such that
h_{k}^{\prime}\leq 0
on
[0,1)
. Then for any
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega;[0,\infty))
, we have
\varphi h^{\prime}_{k}(u)|\nabla u|^{2}\leq 0
. Therefore, letting
k\to\infty
and applying (
7.2
) we can deduce

0\leq\int_{\Omega\cap\{u<1\}}\nabla\varphi\cdot\nabla u\cdot+\varphi\,F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)\qquad\forall\varphi\in C^{1}_{c}(\Omega;[0,\infty))

by means of the dominated convergence theorem. Since
F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(1)=0
and
\nabla u=0
a.e. on
\{u=1\}
, we immediately deduce (
7.5
).

Step three
: We prove that, if
x_{0}\in\Omega
and
r_{0}={\rm dist}(x_{0},\partial\Omega)
, then the function

g(r)=e^{-kr}\,\phi(r)\,,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\phi(r)=\fint_{B_{r}(x_{0})}u\,,\qquad k=\sup_{[0,1]}|F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime\prime}|\,,

is decreasing on
(0,r_{1})
where
r_{1}=\min\{r_{0},2\}
. Indeed, assuming without loss of generality that
x_{0}=0
and testing (
7.5
) with a sequence
\{\varphi_{k}\}\subset C_{c}^{1}(\Omega;[0,\infty))
such that
\varphi_{k}(x)\to[(r^{2}-|x|^{2})/2]_{+}
uniformly and
\nabla\varphi_{k}\to\nabla[(r^{2}-|x|^{2})/2]_{+}
in
L^{2}
yields

\int_{B_{r}}x\cdot\nabla u\leq\int_{B_{r}}\frac{r^{2}-|x|^{2}}{2}|F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)|\leq k\,\frac{r^{2}}{2}\,\int_{B_{r}}u\,,\qquad\forall r<r_{0}\,,

(7.6)

where we have used
F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(0)=0
and the fundamental theorem of calculus to bound
|F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)|\leq ku
. Now, for a.e.
r\in(0,r_{0})
we have that

\phi^{\prime}(r)=\fint_{B_{1}}(y\cdot\nabla u(r\,y))\,dy=\frac{1}{r}\,\fint_{B_{r}}(x\cdot\nabla u(x))\,dx\,.

(7.7)

By combining (
7.6
) with (
7.7
) and using
r_{1}\leq 2
we find
\phi^{\prime}(t)\leq k\,\phi(r)
for a.e.
r<r_{1}
and conclude.

Step four
: We prove that
u
is (Lebesgue equivalent to) a lower semicontinuous function on
\Omega
. Indeed, by step three, we can define
\tilde{u}:\Omega\to[0,1]
by setting

\tilde{u}(x)=\lim_{r\to 0^{+}}e^{-k\,r}\fint_{B_{r}(x)}u\,,\qquad x\in\Omega\,.

Denoting by
\tilde{u}
this limit, we have
\tilde{u}=u
a.e. on
\Omega
by the Lebesgue points theorem. We conclude by proving that
\tilde{u}
is lower semicontinuous: indeed, if
x_{j}\to x\in\Omega
as
j\to\infty
, then

e^{-k\,r}\,\fint_{B_{r}(x)}u=\lim_{j\to\infty}e^{-k\,r}\,\fint_{B_{r}(x_{j})}u\leq\liminf_{j\to\infty}\tilde{u}(x_{j})\,,

where we have used
e^{-k\,r}\fint_{B_{r}(x_{j})}u\leq\tilde{u}(x_{j})
for every
r<\min\{2,{\rm dist}(x_{j},\partial\Omega)\}
. The conclusion follows by letting
r\to 0^{+}
.

Step five
: We prove that for every
\Omega^{\prime}
connected component of
\Omega
, either
u\equiv 0
on
\Omega^{\prime}
or
u>0
on
\Omega^{\prime}
; and that, if
\delta<1
, then
u<1
on
\Omega
.

To prove the first assertion we notice that the lower semicontinuity and the non-negativity of
u
imply that
\{u=0\}
is relatively closed in
\Omega
. At the same time, if
x\in\{u=0\}
, then by step three
0=u(x)\geq e^{-k\,r}\,\fint_{B_{r}(x)}u\geq 0
implies that
u\equiv 0
on
B_{r}(x)
for every
r\leq\min\{{\rm dist}(x,\partial\Omega),2\}
; in particular,
\{u=0\}
is open. Since
\{u=0\}
is both open and relatively closed in
\Omega
, we conclude that
u\equiv 0
on
\Omega^{\prime}
or
u>0
on
\Omega^{\prime}
for any given connected component of
\Omega
.

Next, we show that if
\delta<1
, then
\{u>\delta\}
is open and

2\varepsilon\Delta u=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}W^{\prime}(u)-\lambda V^{\prime}(u)\,,\quad\mbox{as distributions on $\{u>\delta\}$}\,.

(7.8)

By a standard application of the strong maximum principle
[
MR22
, Theorem 6.2]
, (
7.8
) allows us to show that
u<1
on
\Omega
if
\delta<1
, finishing the proof of Theorem
1.3
.(iii). We first notice that
\{u>\delta\}
is open by step four. To prove (
7.8
), the inequality (
7.5
) reduces our task to showing that for every
B_{r}(x)\subset\subset\{u>\delta\}
and every
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{r}(x);[0,\infty))
,

0\geq\int_{\Omega}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi+\varphi\,F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)\,.

(7.9)

And indeed, by lower semicontinuity of
u
, there is
\delta_{0}>0
such that
u\geq\delta+\delta_{0}
on
\mathrm{cl}\,(B_{r}(x))
. In particular, for every
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(B_{r}(x))
with
\varphi\geq 0
there is
\sigma_{0}>0
such that, for every
\sigma\in(0,\sigma_{0}]
,
u_{\sigma}=u-\sigma\,\varphi
takes values in
(\delta,1]
on
B_{r}(x)
, and agrees with
u
on
\Omega\setminus B_{r}(x)
. It is therefore immediate to check that
\{u_{\sigma}^{*}\geq t\}=\{u^{*}\geq t\}
for every
t\in(1/2,\delta)
, so that
\{u_{\sigma}^{*}\geq t\}
is
\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
for every
t\in(1/2,\delta)
. We can then repeat the volume-fixing argument of step one and prove (
7.9
), as desired.

Step six
: We claim that for every
\varphi\in C_{c}^{1}(\Omega)
and
h\in{\rm Lip}_{c}([0,1]\setminus\{\delta\})
, it holds

0=\int_{\Omega}\varphi\,h^{\prime}(u)\,|\nabla u|^{2}+h(u)\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi+\varphi\,h(u)\,F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u).

(7.10)

By virtue of step 5, we can assume without loss of generality that
\varphi
is supported in a connected component of
\Omega
where
u>0
. Since
u
is lower semicontinuous, then
\inf_{\text{supp}(\varphi)}u>0
. So, for
\sigma_{0}
small enough, if
|\sigma|\leq\sigma_{0}
then

\mbox{$u_{\sigma}=u+\sigma\,h(u)\,\varphi$ \, takes values in $[0,1]$}\,.

We wish to test the minimality of
u
against
u_{\sigma}
, which requires verifying the spanning condition and then fixing volumes and testing as in step one. Regarding the spanning condition, if
\delta=1
, then
h\in{\rm Lip}_{c}([0,\delta^{\prime}))
for some
\delta^{\prime}<\delta
, and so
\{u\geq\gamma\}=\{u_{\sigma}\geq\gamma\}
for
\gamma\in[\delta^{\prime},1]
and small enough
\sigma
, implying that
\{u_{\sigma}^{*}\geq t\}
is
\mathcal{C}
-spanning
\mathbf{W}
for every
t\in(1/2,\delta)
. If
\delta<1
, then
(\delta-\eta,\delta+\eta)\subset\text{supp}(h)^{c}
for some
\eta>0
, and so again for small enough
\eta
depending on
\sigma
we find that
\{u\geq\gamma\}=\{u_{\sigma}\geq\gamma\}
for
\delta-\eta/2\leq\gamma\leq\delta+\eta/2
. So we can repeat the volume-fixing argument of step one to obtain (
7.10
).

Step seven:
We finally show that
u
satisfies (
1.16
). By the coarea formula and since
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega)
,
\mathcal{L}^{1}
-a.e.
t_{0}\in(0,\delta_{0})
is a Lebesgue point of
t\to\int_{\{u=t\}}|\nabla u|d\mathcal{H}^{n}
. For such a value of
t_{0}
, let us consider the functions

h_{k}(r)=\begin{cases}1,\quad\qquad\qquad r\in[0,t_{0}-2^{-k}],\\
2^{k}(t_{0}-r),\,\,\quad r\in[t_{0}-2^{-k},t_{0}],\\
0,\quad\qquad\qquad r\in[t_{0},1].\end{cases}

By plugging
h_{k}
into (
7.10
), taking
k\to\infty
, and using the coarea formula, we deduce

\int_{\{u=t_{0}\}}|\nabla u|\,\varphi\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}=\int_{\{u<t_{0}\}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi+\varphi F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)\,.

Integrating between
0
and
\delta
, using the coarea formula and Fubini’s theorem, we find

\displaystyle\int_{\{u<\delta\}}|\nabla u|^{2}\,\varphi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\delta}dt\,\int_{\{u<t\}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi+\varphi F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)

(7.11)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\{u<\delta\}}(\delta-u)\Big(\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi+\varphi F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)\Big)\,.

By analogous reasoning, we deduce

-\int_{\{u=t_{0}\}}|\nabla u|\,\varphi\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}=\int_{\{u>t_{0}\}}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi+\varphi F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)

for a.e.
t_{0}\in(\delta,1)
and thus

\int_{\{u>\delta\}}|\nabla u|^{2}\,\varphi=\int_{\{u>\delta\}}(\delta-u)\Big(\nabla u\cdot\nabla\varphi+\varphi F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)\Big)\,.

(7.12)

By combining (
7.11
) and (
7.12
), we obtain (
1.16
), and complete the proof of the theorem.
∎

We finally prove Proposition
1.4
.

Proof of Proposition
1.4
.

To prove statement (i), let us assume that
u
is continuous in
\Omega
. This implies that
\{u=\delta\}
is closed, and we can thus proceed as in step five to deduce that

\Delta u=F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)\qquad\mbox{on $\{u\neq\delta\}$}\,.

(7.13)

Since
W\in C^{2,1}[0,1]
implies
V\in C^{2,\gamma(n)}[0,1]
with
\gamma(n)=\min\{1,2/n\}
(see
[
MR22
, Appendix 3]
), we have
F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}\in C^{1,\gamma(n)}[0,1]
. The continuity of
u
implies that
\Delta u
is continuous on
\{u\neq\delta\}
, hence that
u\in C^{1,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\{u\neq\delta\})
for every
\alpha<1
. Hence
F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)\in C^{1,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\{u\neq\delta\})
for every
\alpha<\gamma(n)
, and thus
u\in C^{3,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\{u\neq\delta\})
for every
\alpha<\gamma(n)
, as claimed.

Since statement (iii) follows easily from statement (ii), we give a detailed proof of the latter only. We employ an argument similar to
[
ACF84
, Theorem 2.4]
. Given
X\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, let us set

Y=\big\{|\nabla u|^{2}+2\,F_{\varepsilon}(u)\big\}\,X-2\,(X\cdot\nabla u)\,\nabla u\,.

In this way
Y\in C^{1}(\{u\neq\delta\};\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, and by direct computation we find that, on
\{u\neq\delta\}
,

\displaystyle{\rm div}\,\,Y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{|\nabla u|^{2}+2\,F_{\varepsilon}(u)\big\}\,{\rm div}\,\,X-2\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla X[\nabla u]+2\,(X\cdot\nabla u)\,(F_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(u)-\Delta u)

(7.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big\{|\nabla u|^{2}+2\,F_{\varepsilon}(u)\big\}\,{\rm div}\,\,X-2\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla X[\nabla u]\,,

where in the second identity we have used (
7.13
). Now, let us set

\mathcal{L}[X]=\big\{|\nabla u|^{2}+2\,F_{\varepsilon}(u)\big\}\,{\rm div}\,\,X-2\,\nabla u\cdot\nabla X[\nabla u]\,,

so that, thanks to
\nabla u=0

\mathcal{L}^{n+1}
-a.e. on
\{u=\delta\}
, we have

\mathcal{L}[X]\in L^{1}(\Omega)\,,\qquad\mathcal{L}[X]=2\,F_{\varepsilon}(\delta)\,{\rm div}\,\,X\qquad\mbox{$\mathcal{L}^{n+1}$-a.e. on $\{u=\delta\}$}\,.

(7.15)

If we set
S_{t}=\{u>\delta+t\}\cup\{u<\delta-t\}
,
t>0
, then, by the inner variation critical point condition (
7.1
), which gives
\int_{\Omega}\mathcal{L}[X]=0
, and by
{\rm div}\,Y=\mathcal{L}[X]
on
\{u\neq\delta\}
, we find

\int_{S_{t}}{\rm div}\,Y=\int_{S_{t}}\mathcal{L}[X]=-\int_{\{|u-\delta|<t\}}\mathcal{L}[X]

where, by (
7.15
),

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{\{|u-\delta|<t\}}\mathcal{L}[X]\,d\mathcal{L}^{n+1}=\int_{\{u=\delta\}}\mathcal{L}[X]\,d\mathcal{L}^{n+1}=2\,F_{\varepsilon}(\delta)\,\int_{\{u=\delta\}}\,{\rm div}\,X\,d\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\,.

We now write
Y=Y_{1}+Y_{2}
with

Y_{1}=|\nabla u|^{2}\,X-2\,(X\cdot\nabla u)\,\nabla u\,,\qquad Y_{2}=2\,F_{\varepsilon}(u)\,X\,.

Since
u\in W^{1,2}(\Omega)
and
X\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\Omega)
it turns out that
Y_{2}\in W^{1,1}(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
with

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{S_{t}}{\rm div}\,\,Y_{2}=\int_{\Omega}{\rm div}\,\,Y_{2}=0\,.

We have thus proved that

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{S_{t}}{\rm div}\,Y_{1}=2\,F_{\varepsilon}(\delta)\,\int_{\{u=\delta\}}\,{\rm div}\,X\,d\mathcal{L}^{n+1}\,,

(7.16)

where we are stressing that the integral over
\{u=\delta\}
is respect with the Lebesgue measure. Now, for a.e.
t>0
, we have that
S_{t}
is a set of finite perimeter in
\Omega
, with
\partial^{*}S_{t}=\partial^{*}\{u>\delta+t\}\cup\partial^{*}\{u<\delta-t\}
and

\displaystyle\nu_{S_{t}}=-\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\mbox{$\mathcal{H}^{n}$-a.e. on $\partial^{*}\{u>\delta+t\}$}\,,

\displaystyle\nu_{S_{t}}=\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}\,,

\displaystyle\qquad\mbox{$\mathcal{H}^{n}$-a.e. on $\partial^{*}\{u<\delta-t\}$}\,,

and thus

\displaystyle\int_{S_{t}}{\rm div}\,\,Y_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\partial^{*}\{u<\delta-t\}}Y_{1}\cdot\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}-\int_{\partial^{*}\{u>\delta+t\}}Y_{1}\cdot\frac{\nabla u}{|\nabla u|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial^{*}\{u<\delta-t\}}(X\cdot\nabla u)\,|\nabla u|+\int_{\partial^{*}\{u>\delta+t\}}(X\cdot\nabla u)\,|\nabla u|\,.

By (
7.16
), if we assume that
|\{u=\delta\}|=0
, we conclude that

\lim_{t\to 0^{+}}\int_{\partial^{*}\{u<\delta-t\}}(X\cdot\nabla u)\,|\nabla u|-\int_{\partial^{*}\{u>\delta+t\}}(X\cdot\nabla u)\,|\nabla u|=0\,,

(7.17)

with the limit taken with
t
such that
S_{t}
has finite perimeter. This is (
1.17
), and the proof of the proposition is complete.
∎

Appendix A
The diffused interface Euclidean isoperimetric problem

In this appendix we collect some important properties of the diffused interface Euclidean isoperimetric problem considered in
[
MR22
]
, i.e.

\Theta(v,\varepsilon):=\inf\Big\{\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)}{2}:\mathcal{V}(u)=v\Big\}\,,

(A.1)

(where
\mathcal{V}(u)=\mathcal{V}(u;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
and
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)=\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u;\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
), including the uniqueness of minimizers and the characterization of minimizers as the only critical points in the “geometric regime” where
\varepsilon\ll v^{1/(n+1)}
, which is one the main results proved in
[
MR22
]
.

Theorem A.1
.

If
W\in C^{\,2,1}[0,1]
satisfies (
1.9
) and (
1.10
), then the following holds:

(i):
for every
v
and
\varepsilon
positive, there exists a radial decreasing symmetric minimizer
\zeta
of
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)
such that
\zeta\in C^{2,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};(0,1))
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
;

(ii):

\Theta
is continuous on
(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)
with
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)=r^{n}\,\Theta(v/r^{n+1},\varepsilon/r)
for every
r>0
; moreover, for every
\varepsilon>0
, the function
v>0\mapsto\Theta(v,\varepsilon)/v
is strictly decreasing;

(iii):
if
\zeta
is a minimizer of
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)
, then there exists
\Lambda(\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}
such that for all
X\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};\mathbb{R}^{n+1})

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\mathrm{ac}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)\,{\rm div}\,X-2\,\varepsilon\nabla\zeta\,\cdot\,\nabla X[\nabla\zeta]=\Lambda(\zeta)\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}V(\zeta)\,{\rm div}\,X\,,

(A.2)

as well as

2\,\varepsilon^{2}\,\Delta\zeta=W^{\prime}(\zeta)-\varepsilon\,\Lambda(\zeta)\,V^{\prime}(\zeta)\,,\qquad\mbox{on $\mathbb{R}^{n+1}$}\,.

(A.3)

Moreover, for some positive constant
c=c(n,W)
, we have

\Lambda(\zeta)\geq\frac{c}{v^{1/(n+1)}}\,.

(A.4)

(iv):
there is
\sigma_{0}=\sigma_{0}(n,W)>0
such that if
0<\varepsilon<\sigma_{0}\,v^{1/(n+1)}
, then there is a unique modulo translation radial decreasing symmetric minimizer
\zeta_{v,\varepsilon}
of
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)
with maximum at the origin, which satisfies

\Theta(v,\varepsilon)=v^{n/(n+1)}\Big\{(n+1)\,\omega_{n+1}^{1/(n+1)}+{\rm O}_{n,W}\Big(\frac{\varepsilon}{v^{1/(n+1)}}\Big)\Big\}\,,

(A.5)

as
\varepsilon/v^{1/(n+1)}\to 0
.

Proof.

It is convenient to notice that by (
1.9
) there are
\beta_{0}\in(0,1)
and
c_{0}>0
(depending on
W
) such that

\frac{t^{2}}{c_{0}}\geq W(t)\geq c_{0}\,t^{2}\,,\qquad\frac{V(t)}{c_{0}}\geq t\,V^{\prime}(t)\qquad\forall t\in(0,\beta_{0})\,.

(A.6)

Step one
: We prove the existence of
c=c(n,W)
such that, if
u
is a competitor of
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)
and
\beta\in(0,\beta_{0})
, then

\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u;A)}{\mathcal{V}(u;A)}\geq\frac{c}{\varepsilon\,\beta^{2/n}}\,,\qquad\forall A\subset\{0\leq u\leq\beta\}\,.

(A.7)

Indeed, by (
A.6
), if
A\subset\{0\leq u\leq\beta\}
for some
\beta\in(0,\beta_{0})
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u;A)\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,\int_{A}W(u)\geq\frac{c_{0}}{\varepsilon}\,\int_{A}u^{2}\,dx\,,

while
V(t)=(\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{W})^{(n+1)/n}\leq Ct^{2(n+1)/n}
(
t<\beta_{0}
) implies
\mathcal{V}(u;A)\leq C\,\beta^{2/n}\,\int_{A}u^{2}
.

Step two
: We prove
7
7

7

We notice that the analysis performed in
[
MR22
]
, which is focused on uniqueness and stability issues, is limited to the regime where
\varepsilon/v^{1/(n+1)}
is small enough in terms of
n
and
W
.
conclusion (i). By the Pólya-Szegö inequality
[
BZ88
]
we can consider a minimizing sequence
\{u_{j}\}_{j}
of
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)
such that each
u_{j}
is radial decreasing symmetric with respect to the origin. Up to extracting subsequences we can assume that
u_{j}\to\zeta
in
L^{1}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1})
, where
\zeta
is radial decreasing symmetric with respect to the origin and such that
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)\leq 2\,\Theta(v,\varepsilon)
. We prove that
\mathcal{V}(\zeta)=v
, and thus that
\zeta
is a minimizer of
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)
, by showing that

\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{j}\mathcal{V}(u_{j};\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus B_{R})=0\,.

(A.8)

To prove (
A.8
), let us set
u_{j}(x)=g_{j}(|x|)
and
\zeta(x)=g(|x|)
, and notice that, since
g_{j}\to g
a.e. on
(0,\infty)
with
g_{j}
and
g
decreasing on
(0,\infty)
and
g(R)\to 0
as
R\to\infty
, it holds that
\sup_{j}\,g_{j}(R)\to 0
as
R\to\infty
. In particular, for every
R
large enough to ensure
\sup_{j}\,g_{j}(R)\leq\beta_{0}
we can apply (
A.7
) to conclude that

\mathcal{V}(u_{j};\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus B_{R})\leq\frac{\varepsilon\,g_{j}(R)^{2/n}}{c_{0}}\,\sup_{i}\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u_{i})\,,

which implies (
A.8
) thanks (again) to
\sup_{j}\,g_{j}(R)\to 0
as
R\to\infty
. The fact that
\zeta\in C^{2,\alpha}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{n+1};(0,1))
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
is proved as in
[
MR22
, Proof of Theorem 2.1, Step four]
.

Step three
: We prove conclusion (ii). Since the scaling property and the continuity of
\Theta
can be proved as in
[
MR22
, Appendix A]
and
[
MR22
, Step 3, Proof of Theorem 2.1]
, we focus on showing that, for
\varepsilon>0
fixed,
v\mapsto\Theta(v,\varepsilon)/v
is strictly decreasing on
(0,\infty)
. Indeed, by Fubini’s theorem, if
\zeta
is a minimizer for
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)
and we set

Z(t)=\frac{(1/2)\int_{\{x_{1}=t\}}\mathrm{ac}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}}{\int_{\{x_{1}=t\}}V(\zeta)\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}}\,,\qquad t\in\mathbb{R}\,,

then, trivially,
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)\geq v\,\inf_{\mathbb{R}}Z
, with equality if and only if
Z
is constant on
\mathbb{R}
. Since
\zeta
is radial decreasing symmetric, we have
\beta(t)=\sup_{\{x_{1}=t\}}\zeta\to 0
as
t\to\infty
. Since
\mathcal{V}(\zeta)<\infty
, we can find
t_{j}\to\infty
with
\int_{\{x_{1}=t_{j}\}}V(\zeta)\,d\mathcal{H}^{n}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
. Correspondingly,
\beta(t_{j})\to 0^{+}
and, by (
A.7
),
Z(t_{j})\to+\infty
as
j\to\infty
. In particular,
Z
is not constant on
(0,\infty)
, so that
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)>v\,\inf_{\mathbb{R}}Z
, i.e., there is
t_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\Theta(v,\varepsilon)>v\,Z(t_{0})\,.

(A.9)

Now, given
\tilde{v}>v
, if we pick
\delta=(\tilde{v}-v)/(2\,\int_{\{x_{1}=t_{0}\}}V(\zeta))
, decompose
x=(x_{1},x^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\equiv\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, and set

u(x)=\begin{cases}\zeta(t_{0},x^{\prime})\,,&\textup{if }t_{0}-\delta\leq x_{1}\leq t_{0}+\delta\,,\\
\zeta(x_{1}+\delta,x^{\prime})\,,&\textup{if }x_{1}\leq t_{0}-\delta\,,\\
\zeta(x_{1}-\delta,x^{\prime})\,,&\textup{if }x_{1}\geq t_{0}+\delta\,,\end{cases}

then

\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}\big(u;\{|x_{1}-t_{0}|<\delta\}\big)=2\,\delta\,Z(t_{0})\,\int_{\{x_{1}=t_{0}\}}V(\zeta)=Z(t_{0})\,(\tilde{v}-v)\,,

(A.10)

and, similarly,
\mathcal{V}(u)=\mathcal{V}(\zeta)+2\,\delta\,\int_{\{x_{1}=t_{0}\}}V(\zeta)=\tilde{v}
. Since
u
is admissible in
\Theta(\tilde{v},\varepsilon)
, by (
A.10
) we find, as desired,

\frac{\Theta(\tilde{v},\varepsilon)}{\tilde{v}}\leq\frac{\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(u)/2}{\tilde{v}}\leq\frac{\Theta(v,\varepsilon)+Z(t_{0})\,(\tilde{v}-v)}{\tilde{v}}<\frac{\Theta(v,\varepsilon)}{v}\,,

where in the last inequality we have used (
A.9
).

Step four
: The validity of (
A.2
) is immediate from Lemma
4.5
, while (
A.3
) can be deduced from (
A.2
) via integration by parts. To prove (
A.4
) (and thus complete the proof of conclusion (iii)) it is enough to show that, for some constant
C=C(n,W)
, it holds

C\,\Lambda(\zeta)\,v\geq\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)\,.

(A.11)

(Indeed,
\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)/v=\Theta(v,\varepsilon)/v\geq c(n)\,v^{1/(n+1)}
thanks to
\Theta(v,\varepsilon)\geq c(n)\,v^{n/(n+1)}
.) To prove (
A.11
) we first notice that testing (
A.3
) with suitable radial vector fields as done in
[
MR22
, Equation (2.32)]
one finds

\displaystyle(n+1)\,\Lambda(\zeta)\,v

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\frac{W(\zeta)}{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon\,|\nabla\zeta|^{2}

(A.12)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle(n-1)\,\mathcal{AC}_{\varepsilon}(\zeta)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\frac{W(\zeta)}{\varepsilon}\,.

Clearly (
A.12
) implies (
A.11
) when
n\geq 2
, but leaves open the case
n=1
; however, it always ensures that
\Lambda(\zeta)\geq 0
. Next, we notice that by testing (
A.3
) with
\varphi=\zeta\,\phi_{k}^{2}
for
\phi_{k}\in C_{c}^{\infty}(B_{k+1};[0,1])
with
\phi_{k}=1
on
B_{k}
and
{\rm Lip}(\phi_{k})\leq 2
for every
k
, and keeping in mind that
V^{\prime}\geq 0
and that
\Lambda(\zeta)\geq 0
, we find

2\,\varepsilon\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\phi_{k}^{2}\,|\nabla\zeta|^{2}\leq 2\,\varepsilon\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\phi_{k}\,\zeta\,|\nabla\zeta|\,|\nabla\phi_{k}|+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\frac{W^{\prime}(\zeta)\,\zeta\,\phi_{k}}{\varepsilon}+\Lambda(\zeta)\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}V^{\prime}(\zeta)\zeta\,.

Since for
k\geq k(\zeta)
we have
\zeta\leq\beta_{0}
on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\setminus B_{k}
, and we can thus use (
A.6
) and the Cauchy–Schwartz inequality to deduce, for some
C=C(W)
,

\displaystyle\varepsilon\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\phi_{k}^{2}|\nabla\zeta|^{2}\leq C\,\varepsilon\,\int_{\{\zeta<\beta_{0}\}}\zeta^{2}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\frac{[W^{\prime}(\zeta)]^{+}\,\zeta}{\varepsilon}+C\,\Lambda(\zeta)\,\mathcal{V}(\zeta)\,,

(A.13)

where we have used
c_{0}\,t\,V^{\prime}(t)\leq t^{2(n+1)/n}\leq V(t)/c_{0}
for every
t\in(0,1)
(which, in turns, easily follows from
W(t)\geq c_{0}\,t^{2}
on
(0,\beta_{0})
and
W(t)\leq t^{2}/c_{0}
on
(0,1)
). Finally, by (
1.9
), and up to decreasing the value of
\beta_{0}
, we have
W^{\prime}<0
on
(1-\beta_{0},1)
. Using this fact in combination with
\inf_{(\beta_{0},1-\beta_{0})}W>0
and
t\,W^{\prime}(t)\leq W(t)/c_{0}
for
t\in(0,\beta_{0})
, we see that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}[W^{\prime}(\zeta)]^{+}\,\zeta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\{\zeta<\beta_{0}\}}W^{\prime}(\zeta)\,\zeta+{\rm Lip}(W)\,\int_{\{\beta_{0}\leq\zeta\leq 1-\beta_{0}\}}\,\zeta

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\Big\{\frac{1}{c_{0}}+\frac{{\rm Lip}(W)\,(1-\beta_{0})}{\inf_{(\beta_{0},1-\beta_{0})}W}\Big\}\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}W(\zeta)\,,

and thus conclude from (
A.13
) that

\displaystyle\varepsilon\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\phi_{k}^{2}\,|\nabla\zeta|^{2}\leq C\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}\frac{W(\zeta)}{\varepsilon}+C\,\Lambda(\zeta)\,v\,.

By letting
k\to\infty
, by adding
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}W(\zeta)/\varepsilon
to both sides of this inequality, and by noticing that (
A.12
) implies
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{n+1}}W(\zeta)/\varepsilon\leq C(n)\,\Lambda(\zeta)\,v
for every
n\geq 1
, we conclude the proof of (
A.11
).

Step five
: The outer form of the Euler–Lagrange equation (
A.3
) follows from Lemma
4.5
, and a classical computation (based on integration by parts made possible by the
C^{2}
-regularity of
\zeta
) allows one to derive (
A.2
) from (
A.3
). This completes the proof of conclusion (iii). Conclusions (iv) is contained in
[
MR22
, Theorem 1.1]
.
∎
